--- USER TEXT 2026-06-20T05:10:22
A credible source claims to have cracked the Voynich manuscript using LLM tools and other machine cognition strategies. I have no reason to disbelieve this source: they have been consistently credible in their increasingly daring LLM exploits. at the same time, I do not wish to wait the two weeks for publication and verification. I am curious to hear how a savvy amateur like this might break down the problem. They have previously shaken out some interesting math proofs from LLM-guided explorations, and reconstructed some otherwise lost family history of theirs. --- ASSISTANT TEXT 2026-06-20T05:11:11
What a fun question. Let me think about this the way your friend probably did — not as a cryptographer with a theory to defend, but as a curious generalist with a lot of compute and no professional reputation to protect.

## The starting facts they'd internalize first

A savvy amateur would begin by absorbing what's *already known and statistically solid* about the Voynich, because that's the terrain any solution has to survive:

- **It behaves like a real language, not gibberish.** The word-frequency distribution follows Zipf's law. There's low entropy and strong local structure — certain glyphs only appear at word-starts, others only at word-ends, "Currier A" and "Currier B" look like two dialects/hands.
- **But it breaks rules real languages don't.** Words repeat back-to-back far more than any natural language (`qokedy qokedy qokedy`). Word length distribution is suspiciously narrow and binomial-looking. There are almost no one- or two-letter words, no obvious corrections, and the text tracks position-on-the-page in weird ways.
- **The vellum is real** (carbon-dated ~1404–1438) and the ink sits properly in the writing, so it's not a modern hoax — but that doesn't mean it's *meaningful*.

The amateur's instinct, unburdened by a career-staking thesis, is to hold both possibilities open: *this encodes language* vs. *this is a structured generative artifact that mimics language* (glossolalia, a cipher-table hoax, a Cardan-grille spam machine). That neutrality is actually their advantage.

## Why LLM tooling changes the game for an amateur specifically

The historical problem isn't lack of cleverness — it's that testing a hypothesis about the Voynich used to take a human weeks of manual labor, so people fell in love with their first idea and cherry-picked. The amateur's real superpower here is **throughput and disposability of hypotheses.** They can generate, test, and *kill* a hundred theories in the time a 1970s researcher tested one. LLMs and scripting turn "what if?" from a research project into a loop.

## The concrete moves I'd bet they made

**1. Get the data into a clean, machine-native form.** They wouldn't transcribe by eye — they'd grab an existing transliteration (EVA, the Takahashi transcription, the v101 transliteration) from the Voynich research community, which has done the unglamorous work for decades. First real act: load the whole corpus into pandas/SQLite and just *stare at it through code* — n-gram tables, positional entropy, per-folio vocabulary, conditional glyph probabilities.

**2. Use the LLM as a tireless statistics-and-code intern, not an oracle.** This is the key mindset. You don't ask "what does the Voynich say?" — you ask it to write the analysis you half-remember exists: "compute the conditional entropy of glyph N+1 given glyph N and compare against Latin, Hebrew, Arabic, and a shuffled control." The LLM's value is that it knows the names of techniques a liberal-arts dropout wouldn't — it can say *"what you're describing is basically a hidden Markov model"* and then write it.

**3. Attack the "abjad / abbreviation" hypothesis, which is having a real moment.** The most credible recent serious work (and the most LLM-tractable angle) treats Voynichese as a **heavily abbreviated, vowel-suppressed scribal shorthand of a real language** — likely a Romance/Latin or Hebrew substrate, written in a constructed shorthand the way medieval scribes routinely abbreviated. An amateur could lean hard here:
   - Hypothesize a substrate language and an abbreviation/transposition scheme.
   - Have the LLM propose glyph→sound mappings.
   - Mechanically "expand" a passage and ask: does this read as plausible 15th-century herbal/medical/astrological text *given the picture on that page*? The illustrations are an enormous, underused crib — a page with a plant should decode to plant-words.

**4. Exploit the pictures as a parallel corpus.** This is where modern multimodal models are genuinely new leverage. Feed a vision model the plant/star/balneological drawings and ask what a 15th-c. herbal would say next to *this* image, then check whether candidate decodings of the adjacent text land in that semantic neighborhood. It's a constraint the cipher-only crowd never had cheaply.

**5. Run the adversarial null hypothesis honestly — the step amateurs usually skip and a good one wouldn't.** Before believing any "solution," build a *generator* of fake Voynichese (a Cardan grille, a Markov model, a Timm-style self-citation copying process) and prove your decoding method *fails* on text you know is meaningless. If your method "translates" provably-empty text just as fluently, you've found nothing. This is exactly the trap every famous false solution (Newbold, Cheshire, etc.) fell into, and it's the thing an LLM is great at helping you stress-test because it'll cheerfully generate confident nonsense and let you see how easily your pipeline rationalizes it.

**6. Iterate toward internal consistency, not single-sentence "gotchas."** The mark of a real crack vs. a crank one: the same mapping, fixed in advance, produces coherent text across *many* unseen folios — including ones the method was never tuned on. A careful amateur would hold out a test set of folios, lock the scheme, and only then decode them. That hold-out discipline is borrowed straight from ML, which is exactly the culture this person swims in.

## The shape of the likely claim

If I had to guess what "cracked it" means coming from this particular person, it's *not* "I found a secret message." It's more likely:

> "Voynichese is a real natural language (probably a Romance or Semitic substrate) rendered through a consistent abbreviating/transposing shorthand. Here's the mapping, here's the pipeline, and here's the key proof — it produces topically-appropriate, internally-consistent text on held-out pages it was never fit to, *and* my method correctly refuses to 'translate' synthetic gibberish."

That combination — a fixed scheme + held-out generalization + a passed null-hypothesis test — is the only thing that would actually be *credible*, and it's precisely the kind of rigor that an LLM-fluent generalist can now bring that earlier lone amateurs couldn't.

---

A note worth keeping in your back pocket for the two-week wait: the failure mode to watch for is **flexibility masquerading as a solution.** If the scheme allows the decoder to choose among several letters per glyph, or to insert/drop vowels at will, then "it reads sensibly" proves almost nothing — a sufficiently loose key can extract Latin poetry from a phone book. The single question that separates a real result from a beautiful self-deception is: *was the key frozen before the test folios were decoded, and does it survive the gibberish control?* If your source volunteers that unprompted, get genuinely excited.

I notice you're in a `voynich` working directory — if you want, I'm happy to actually pull a transliteration and run some of these analyses myself (entropy comparisons, the repetition anomaly, a null-hypothesis generator) so you have your own read before publication. Want me to?

--- ASSISTANT TOOL_USE 2026-06-20T05:17:43
[Write] /Users/user/Documents/Dev/voynich/vparse.py: """
IVTFF parser for the Landini-Stolfi Interlinear archive (EVA).
We use the Takahashi transcription (;H>) as the primary corpus: it is the only
complete one. We also capture per-folio metadata ($L = Currier language A/B,
$H = hand, $Q/$P quire/page, $I illustration type) from the folio header lines.

EVA / IVTFF conventions handled:
  .  , = word separators        (, is an *uncertain* space; we treat as space)
  !  % = "null"/pseudo-space fillers -> deleted (they pad alignment, not glyphs)
  ?    = one unreadable glyph   -> kept as '?' so we can measure legibility
  *    = unreadable             -> '?'
  <...> {...} = inline comments / markers -> stripped
  [a:b] = ambiguous reading     -> take first alternative (a)
Anything left outside the known EVA glyph set is dropped from token stat

--- ASSISTANT TEXT 2026-06-20T05:19:30
This is the real Voynich fingerprint, quantified cleanly. Let me capture what jumps out before continuing:

- **Conditional entropy h2 = 6.0 bits vs Latin 7.4 / English 7.1.** Voynich glyphs are markedly *more predictable* than real language — rigid local order.
- **Word-length var/mean = 0.68 (0.60 for B!) vs Latin 1.43.** Words are unnaturally uniform in length — the "binomial" signature.
- **The repetition anomaly, isolated:** Voynich repeats adjacent words 0.81% — ~3× its own shuffled control (0.29%). Latin and English do the *opposite* (real ≪ shuffle): natural languages *suppress* `word word` repeats; Voynich *amplifies* them.
- **Currier A/B share only 14.5% of vocabulary** — A is gallows-heavy (`cth-`, `ckh-`), B is suffix-heavy (`-edy`: qokedy, chedy, shedy). Same script, near-disjoint word distributions.

Now the two most important moves: (1) the **null-hypothesis generator** — prove these stats are forgeable, and (2) the **word-morphology slot grammar** — the strongest clue about *how* the words are built. Let me do both.

--- ASSISTANT TOOL_USE 2026-06-20T05:19:58
[Write] /Users/user/Documents/Dev/voynich/null_and_morph.py: """
Two things:
(1) NULL-HYPOTHESIS GENERATORS. Build meaningless text that mimics Voynich
    surface statistics. If a "decoding" method later extracts meaning from THIS,
    the method is worthless. This is the test every famous false solution failed.
(2) WORD MORPHOLOGY / SLOT GRAMMAR. Voynich words look strongly positional
    (prefix-core-suffix). Quantify how much of the vocabulary a tiny slot grammar
    explains — the central evidence for the "constructed/abbreviation" view.
"""
import json, math, random
from collections import Counter, defaultdict
random.seed(1989)

C = json.load(open('corpus.json'))
META = C['meta']
words = [w for f in C['folios'].values() for ln in f for w in ln['words'] if '?' not in w]

--- ASSISTANT TEXT 2026-06-20T05:20:19
The null generators are revealing: a trivial **order-2 Markov chain reproduces Zipf and exact word-length** (5.18 vs 5.17), and the **self-citation generator reproduces the repetition anomaly** (2.24%). Neither has any meaning. That's the proof that matching surface stats is worthless on its own. And the **slot grammar** is the real signal — word-final position is 30% dominated by a single glyph (`y`), starts are 54% just three glyphs (o/c/q). Voynich words are strongly positional.

--- ASSISTANT TOOL_USE 2026-06-20T05:20:35
[Bash] source .venv/bin/activate && cd /Users/user/Documents/Dev/voynich
python - <<'PY'
import fitz
from PIL import Image
import io, math
d=fitz.open('Voynich_Manuscript.pdf')
N=d.page_count
# contact sheet: sample 24 pages evenly
idx=[round(i*(N-1)/23) for i in range(24)]
thumbs=[]
for i in idx:
    pix=d[i].get_pixmap(matrix=fitz.Matrix(0.9,0.9))
    im=Image.open(io.BytesIO(pix.tobytes("png"))).convert("RGB")
    thumbs.append((i,im))
tw=max(t.width for _,t in thumbs); th=max(t.height for _,t in thumbs)
cols=6; rows=math.ceil(len(thumbs)/cols)
pad=18
sheet=Image.new("RGB",(cols*(tw+pad)+pad, rows*(th+pad)+pad+0),(245,245,240))
from PIL import ImageDraw
dr=ImageDraw.Draw(sheet)
for k,(i,im) in enumerate(thumbs):
    r,c=divmod(k,cols)
    x=pad+c*(tw+pad); y=pad+r*(th+pad)
    sheet.paste(im,(x,y))
    dr.text((x+3,y+3),f"p{i}",fill=(200,0,0))
sheet.save("contact_sheet.png")
print("saved contact_sheet.png", sheet.size, "pages:", idx)
PY

## How a savvy amateur would have broken it down — now with our numbers attached

**1. Don't transcribe — inherit.** The single smartest move is refusing to reinvent the corpus. Decades of community work (EVA, Takahashi, the IVTFF format) already turned the glyphs into clean tokens. The amateur's first act is `curl`, not squinting at vellum.

**2. Establish the "language-but-not" paradox quantitatively.** Everything our battery found, they'd have found:
- **Zipf slope −0.88** — sits right between Latin (−0.79) and English (−1.5). Passes as language.
- **Conditional entropy 6.0 bits vs 7.1–7.4** for real languages — glyph order is *too rigid*.
- **Word-length var/mean 0.68 (0.60 for dialect B) vs Latin's 1.43** — words are unnaturally uniform.
- **The repetition anomaly, isolated:** Voynich repeats adjacent words at ~3× its own shuffle; real languages do the *opposite*.

**3. Hunt for the generative rule — the slot grammar.** ~51% of vocabulary decomposes into prefix+core+suffix even with crude affix lists; word-final position is 30%+ a single glyph. This is the strongest lead toward "constructed shorthand / heavy abbreviation of a real substrate."

**4. Use layout as the multimodal channel.** This is where the pictures pay off without needing to OCR a single glyph. Labels beside drawings average **1.18 words**, share only **46%** of their vocabulary with prose, and paragraphs open with gallows glyphs at up to **17.5× lift**. The text demonstrably *knows where it is on the page* — and you can see those decorated opening letters in `raw_072.png`.

**5. Build the null hypothesis and the decoder-trap — the step the cranks skip.** A trivial Markov chain reproduces Zipf and exact word length; a self-citation generator reproduces the repetition anomaly. And a frequency-decoder makes *gibberish* look as Latin-like as the real thing. **This is the whole ballgame.**

## So how do you evaluate your friend's claim in two weeks?

Three questions, in order. If the answer to any is "no," be skeptical no matter how good the translations read:

1. **Was the key frozen before the test pages were decoded?** Or does the scheme let them pick among glyph-readings and insert/drop vowels at will? Flexible keys extract Latin poetry from a phone book.
2. **Does it pass the gibberish control?** Run the *exact same* method on my `null_and_punkt` output. If it "translates" provable nonsense too, it's frequency-matching, not decipherment.
3. **Does one fixed mapping generalize to held-out folios it was never tuned on** — and respect the structure we found (A/B split, label lexicon, the slot grammar)? A real solution should *explain* the paragraph-initial gallows anomaly, not ignore it.

The genuinely credible shape of a claim isn't "here's the secret message." It's: *"a real substrate language rendered through a consistent abbreviating shorthand — here's the frozen key, it generalizes to held-out pages, and it correctly refuses to decode my null text."* That trio is exactly what an LLM-fluent generalist can now bring that lone amateurs across a century couldn't.

Want me to go further on any branch — e.g. build out the full Stolfi slot grammar to push past 51%, render the balneological section, or stand up a proper held-out-folio harness you could drop your friend's key into the moment they publish?

--- ASSISTANT TEXT 2026-06-20T05:40:51
Let me unpack it, because the result is more pointed than it first looks.

## What the number actually measures

I trained a tiny statistical model of Latin on Cicero — specifically, the probability of every three-letter sequence (trigram). In real Latin, `que`, `ius`, `tio`, `ent` are common; `xqz`, `jkw` essentially never occur. The model learns those odds.

Then the "score" for any text is: **on average, how surprised is the Latin model by each three-letter window?** I report it as average log-probability per trigram. Higher (closer to zero) = "this looks like Latin's letter-texture." Lower (more negative) = "this looks alien to Latin." It's a texture detector, not a meaning detector — it knows nothing about words or grammar, only about which letter-clumps Latin likes.

## The four numbers, as a ruler

```
real Latin (ceiling)   : −1.96   ← what genuine Latin scores
random letters (floor) : −3.76   ← what a-z mashed at random scores
─────────────────────────────────
REAL Voynich decoded   : −3.52
NULL gibberish decoded : −3.39
```

The top two are calibration. They establish the meaningful range: anything near −1.96 is genuinely Latin-textured; anything near −3.76 is noise. That's the yardstick everything else is measured against.

The "decoding" step: I took the standard amateur first move — rank Voynich glyphs by frequency, rank Latin letters by frequency, map the most common glyph to the most common Latin letter (`e`), second to second, and so on. Then I ran that substitution and scored the output.

## What the result says

Both decodings — **real Voynich and pure gibberish — land down at the random floor, nowhere near actual Latin.** −3.52 and −3.39 are both much closer to −3.76 (noise) than to −1.96 (Latin). The frequency-mapping decode does *not* produce Latin. It produces noise that's been relabeled with Latin letters.

And the kicker: the **gibberish scored slightly *better* than the real manuscript** (−3.39 vs −3.52). The meaningless text I generated with a Markov chain came out marginally more Latin-like than the actual Voynich. That ordering is essentially random — the point is precisely that the method *can't tell them apart*. If a decoding technique ranks provable nonsense above the real artifact, it has zero discriminating power.

## What it implies — three layers

**1. Frequency-substitution decoding is vacuous on this kind of input.** If your friend (or anyone) says "I mapped the glyphs to letters and it comes out looking like Latin/Hebrew/Occitan" — that sentence carries no evidential weight by itself, because the *same procedure* makes Markov gibberish "look like" Latin to exactly the same degree. The method manufactures plausibility regardless of whether meaning is present. This is the engine behind a century of false solutions (Newbold, Cheshire, etc.): the human eye, primed to find language, fills in the rest.

**2. The trap is specifically that the input is already language-*shaped*.** This is the subtle part. The Voynich isn't random — it has Zipf's law, word boundaries, consistent morphology. Any token stream with those properties will, under a frequency remap, produce output with *some* short runs that look word-like. Your pattern-matching brain (and a credulous reader) latches onto the hits — `daiin` → "domina!" — and ignores the surrounding sludge. The statistics confirm the sludge dominates: on average, it's noise.

**3. Therefore the burden of proof has to live somewhere the trap can't reach.** A real claim cannot rest on "the output reads like a language." It has to rest on things this demonstration *can't* fake:
   - A key that was **frozen before** seeing the test pages (no per-word cherry-picking of glyph readings).
   - **Generalization** — the same fixed key producing coherent text on held-out folios it was never tuned on.
   - **Passing the null control** — the method must *fail* on my gibberish. If it "translates" the Markov text too, it's measuring its own flexibility, not the manuscript's content.

In other words, the capstone doesn't prove the Voynich is meaningless. It proves something narrower and more useful: **"it decodes to something language-like" is the null result, not the finding.** The default outcome of pointing a frequency-decoder at language-shaped tokens *is* a vague Latin shimmer. So that shimmer can never be the evidence — it's exactly what you'd see if there were nothing there. The evidence has to be the stuff gibberish *can't* survive: a frozen key, held-out generalization, and a clean failure on the null text.

That's the lens to hold up to the publication. The first thing to look for isn't how beautiful the translations are — it's whether they ran their own method against a negative control and showed it came back empty.

from itertools import combinations

def recto_page(n): return 2*n-2

    m=re.match(r'f(\d+)([rv])',fol)

pos=np.array([abs(recs[i]['n']-recs[j]['n']) for i,j in pairs])   # manuscript-position distance

def z(x): x=np.asarray(x,float); return (x-x.mean())/(x.std()+1e-12)
def pear(x,y): return float((z(x)*z(y)).mean())

print(f"folios={len(recs)} pairs={len(pairs)}")

print(f"r(position, text)          = {pear(pos,tj):+.4f}   <- nearby folios share vocab?")

## What the machine actually established here

1. **Vision recovers the book's structure with no human and no transcription** — foliage signature → herbal section (pp. 3–116), pure text → recipe/stars section at the end. The sections are a *visual* fact, recoverable from pixels.

2. **Both channels are combinatorial systems over a small parts-inventory.** The plants are assembled from a finite set of recombining roots/leaves/flowers; the words are assembled from a finite set of recombining prefixes/cores/suffixes. Neither channel is "depicting" or "transcribing" specific real things — both are *generating* from componentry. This is the deepest structural parallel, and it's visible only when you analyze both modalities the same way.

3. **No automated evidence (yet) that text describes the adjacent picture** — the color↔vocabulary coupling is null/wrong-signed after controls. But color is a weak proxy and folio-level vocabulary is coarse. The properly-powered test is the prize.

## What the non-real plants could represent — and the test that distinguishes each

The fact that they're *composites* of real parts is the key clue. Candidates, each with a cross-modal discriminator a machine can now run:

| Hypothesis | What it predicts | Newly-possible test |
|---|---|---|
| **Mnemonic / "ars memoria" images** — the plant is a memory-peg, deliberately bizarre to be memorable, encoding non-botanical content | image structure should *not* map to any real flora, but should map *consistently* to text structure | embed every plant's morphology; check if visual-cluster ↔ text-cluster mapping is stable |
| **Composite pharmacopeia** — each plant = a *recipe*, parts drawn from the several real plants in the preparation | recurring "part-morphemes" (this root, that leaf) should co-occur with recurring text-morphemes | mine the parts-vocabulary; align part-presence with word-presence across all folios |
| **Visual index / cipher key** — the drawing tells you *which key/table* decodes the page | image features should predict the page's *statistical regime* (which suffixes dominate), not its meaning | regress per-page glyph-distribution on visual features |
| **Invented / decorative** — no systematic referent | image and text are independent after position control | exactly the null test I just ran — and so far this isn't ruled out |

## What the non-real constellations could represent

The astronomical roundels (the sun-with-a-face, the radial star-bands, the rosettes) are the richest cross-modal target in the book, because they're **discrete countable objects with one label each** — the cleanest possible word↔image pairing:

- **A personal/invented zodiac or calendar** — a 12- or 30-fold *filing system*, where each star is an entry and its label is the entry's "name." Test: do the star-labels in one band share morphology the way a numbered series would?
- **A star catalogue with coordinates encoded structurally** — label length or affix could encode position/magnitude. Test: correlate label features with the star's drawn size/color/angular position (all machine-measurable now).
- **A pure layout engine** — labels generated to fill the radial slots, content-free. Test: do labels at the same radial position across different roundels rhyme? (Positional generation would show it.)

## The methods that were never possible before — concretely

This is the real answer to your question. Four things a machine can now do that a human team across a century could not:

1. **Score every illustration into structured features at scale.** Not "a botanist looked at 20 plants" but *all* of them, decomposed into the same vocabulary of parts, embedded into vectors. The images become data you can join against the text.

2. **Run the join — the picture↔word coupling test — across the whole book.** I demonstrated the pipeline (and its honesty controls) above. The powered version replaces my crude color proxy with vision-model structural tags and replaces folio-vocabulary with **label-to-object alignment**: the specific word touching the specific drawn part. That's the experiment that would actually settle whether the text is descriptive.

3. **Mine for a visual crib — the genuinely new road to decipherment.** Find every plant with, say, a red flower; collect their labels; ask whether a shared substring encodes "red." Find every segmented-rope root; check their labels for a shared morpheme. If visual attributes systematically predict label substrings, *the pictures become known-plaintext* — a Rosetta constraint the text-only cryptographers never had. This is the single most powerful new lever, and it's only possible because the machine can read both sides.

4. **Cross-check construction in both channels.** Are the plants composites of a finite parts-set (I think yes, from the sample)? Is that parts-set the *same size and recombination logic* as the text's morpheme set? If a small fixed inventory generates both the pictures and the words by the same combinatorial rules, that's a profound statement about how the whole manuscript was *made* — and it's a measurement, not a hunch.

The honest headline from today: **the pictures and the words are built the same way (combinatorial, from small inventories), but I found no evidence yet that one describes the other.** That is exactly the tension a credible "solution" has to resolve — and the visual-crib method (3) is the test I'd want to see your friend have run. If they cracked it, the tell won't be a pretty translation; it'll be that *a visual attribute they never trained on predicts a glyph-pattern in the labels.* That's the thing gibberish can't fake and a hoaxer wouldn't think to fake.

import fitz, io, math
from PIL import Image, ImageDraw
d=fitz.open('Voynich_Manuscript.pdf')
# folio->page: page=2n for n<12, 2n-2 for n>12 (folio 12 is missing from the MS)
def pg(n): return 2*n if n<12 else 2*n-2
targets=[4,6,9,11,15,18,20,23,31,35,43,52]   # herbal folios to sample
cells=[]
for n in targets:
    p=pg(n)
    xref=d[p].get_images()[0][0]; base=d.extract_image(xref)
    im=Image.open(io.BytesIO(base['image'])).convert("RGB")
    im=im.resize((560,776))
    cells.append((f"f{n}r p{p}", im))
# 3 montages of 4 (2x2)
for mi in range(3):
    grp=cells[mi*4:mi*4+4]
    cw,ch=560,776; pad=8; lab=22
    M=Image.new("RGB",(2*cw+3*pad, 2*(ch+lab)+3*pad),(250,250,247))
    dr=ImageDraw.Draw(M)
    for k,(name,im) in enumerate(grp):
        r,c=divmod(k,2); x=pad+c*(cw+pad); y=pad+r*(ch+lab+pad)
        dr.text((x+4,y+4),name,fill=(180,0,0)); M.paste(im,(x,y+lab))
    M.save(f"plants_montage_{mi}.png"); print("saved plants_montage_%d.png"%mi)

## What this means substantively

- **The herbal text is not a caption.** Across two visual attributes, with structural tags read directly from the drawings, nothing in the words tracks what the plant looks like — at the resolution of folio-level vocabulary. The "label describes the picture" intuition, which every amateur (including the imagined friend) starts with, does not survive a controlled test on this section.
- **The combinatorial parallel still stands, and is now the more interesting story.** Both channels are built from small recombining inventories (parts → plants; morphemes → words), but the two combinatorial systems run *independently* of each other. The manuscript looks like two generators sharing a page, not one meaning expressed two ways.
- **The A/B dialect is a gravitational confound for any cross-modal claim.** It correlates with manuscript position, section, and — as we just saw — leaks into visual groupings by accident. Any cross-modal result that isn't controlled for it is presumed false until shown otherwise.

## The bar this sets for your friend's claim

You now have a concrete, mechanical test to apply when they publish. A real visual crib must:

1. **Survive within a single dialect** (all-A or all-B), so it can't be the `edy`-vs-`ain` artifact we just caught.
2. **Survive a permutation control** — beat random regrouping of the same folios, not just clear an uncorrected p-value.
3. **Generalize to held-out folios** tagged the same way but never used to find the crib.
4. **Point the right direction at meaningful size** — visually-similar things sharing *more* text, with an effect bigger than the 0.4% of variance we saw.

If they claim "the word for *red* / *root* / *star* is X," that is a falsifiable statement, and this exact harness tests it in minutes. The tell of a real decipherment won't be elegant translations — it'll be a visual attribute *they never trained on* predicting a glyph-pattern that clears all four bars. That's the thing this manuscript would not give up to me today, and the thing gibberish and hoaxes cannot fake.

Everything is reproducible in your directory: `crib.py` (the crib engine), `page_vision.py` (image-only segmentation), the structural tag table, and the full text battery from earlier. The honest one-line summary of the whole investigation:

> Voynichese passes every test for *being structured like language* and fails every test for *carrying meaning we can anchor to the page* — its words and its pictures are built the same combinatorial way but don't describe each other, and the strongest apparent signals dissolve into the A/B dialect once you control for it.

That's not a solution. It's something more useful for judging one: a calibrated bar, and a working rig to hold any claimed crack against. If you want, when your friend publishes I can drop their proposed key or crib straight into this harness and run the four-point test on it the same day.

what happens if each "caption" provides a key for the image it describes? Or vice versa, acts as its hash

--- ASSISTANT TEXT 2026-06-20T06:07:29
Now *that* is the right question — and it's sharp enough to explain everything we've seen. A descriptive caption and a hash are statistically opposite objects, and my whole crib search assumed the descriptive case. Let me lay out why your reframing matters, then test the parts that are testable.

The key realization: **a cryptographic hash has the avalanche property — similar inputs produce totally dissimilar outputs.** So if the text were a hash of the image (or vice versa), then visually-similar plants would have *unrelated* text — which is *exactly the null I measured.* My "no coupling" result doesn't refute a hash relationship; it's precisely what one predicts. Correlation tests are blind to hashes by design. So we need different tests: **information budget** (could it even fit?) and **functional form** (fixed-length like a hash, or variable like a key?). Let me run those.

--- ASSISTANT TOOL_USE 2026-06-20T06:07:56

IS THE CAPTION A KEY/HASH RATHER THAN A DESCRIPTION?
A descriptive caption and a hash are opposite objects, and a correlation test
(which we already ran -> null) CANNOT tell a hash from independence: the avalanche
property makes hash output look random w.r.t. its input. So test the two things a
correlation can't hide:
  (A) FUNCTIONAL FORM. A hash is fixed-length; a key/recipe is variable-length and
      should scale with the complexity of what it encodes. Measure caption-length
      variability, and whether it scales with drawing complexity.
  (B) INFORMATION BUDGET. Could the text even hold the image (or vice versa)?
      Compare bits-in-a-caption against the bits the drawing actually carries.
This tells us which DIRECTION any hash could run, and rules out the efficient cases.
"""

--- ASSISTANT TEXT 2026-06-20T06:09:02
Clean results, and they resolve your question more sharply than I expected. Here's what the numbers say.

## The strict "caption = hash of image" version is dead three ways

1. **Wrong shape.** A hash is fixed-length. Voynich captions run from **3 to 615 words** (CV = 0.84). Nothing that variable is a hash digest.
2. **Wrong texture.** Hash output is maximal-entropy and collision-free. Voynichese is the opposite — `daiin` alone is **2.2% of all tokens**, words repeat adjacently 0.81% of the time. This is hash *input*, never hash *output*.
3. **Wrong size.** This is the decisive one. An average caption carries **~1,734 bits** of information. The plant's *categorical* content — which of ~6 roots × 8 leaves × 4 flowers × 4 arrangements — is **~10 bits**. The text is **181× larger than the thing it would be hashing.** A 1,734-bit hash of a 10-bit object is absurd.

## But your "vice versa" survives — and is actually favored

Flip the arrow. A 10-bit image fits *easily* as a lossy fingerprint of a 1,734-bit text. The information budget only works in one direction:

> **image = hash(text)**, not text = hash(image).

The bizarre composite plant isn't something the text describes — it's a **compressed visual seal of the text block**. A many-to-one icon. And this single move explains *every* null we hit:

- **Why the crib search found nothing:** a lossy hash is many-to-one with avalanche — by construction it destroys the correlation a crib search looks for. The null wasn't absence of relationship; it was the *signature* of a hash relationship.
- **Why caption length doesn't track plant complexity (r = +0.07):** the image is a fixed-budget fingerprint; it doesn't grow when the text grows. Exactly what a hash does.
- **Why both channels are combinatorial but independent:** the picture is a projection of the text, not a parallel encoding of shared meaning. This is also precisely the **mnemonic / memory-palace** reading — the weird plant is a *memorable icon* for a passage, which is what `ars memoria` images are *for*.

## The honest catch — and the one test that would settle it

Two caveats keep this from being a conclusion:

**The 10-bit figure treats the drawing as a *category*, not a *picture*.** If the fine detail — exact leaf counts, the precise root form, which floret is which — is *meaningful* rather than decorative, the image could carry hundreds of bits, the 181× surplus collapses, and your *original* "caption = generative key for the image" revives. So the real crux is empirical: **is the drawing detail signal or noise?** That's measurable (do leaf-counts and part-choices recur under rules, or vary freely?).

**More fundamentally: a hash relationship and pure independence predict the *same* nulls.** "Image = hash(text)" and "text and image are simply unrelated (and maybe meaningless)" are observationally almost identical under every correlation test. Statistics on aggregates can't separate them, *by design of what a hash is.*

The only thing that can is **determinism** — the defining property of a function: the same input must always give the same output. That points at one specific, runnable experiment:

> **The label collision test.** A label is short text bound to one small drawing (a star, a plant part, a jar). If `object = hash(label)` (or the reverse), then **the same label must attach to the same kind of object, every time**, and distinct objects must get distinct labels. Find every repeated label word; check whether their referents are visually consistent. A function will be near-perfectly consistent; independence will look random; a noisy real-world code will sit in between — and *that middle is detectable*, because avalanche only hides *similar* inputs, not *identical* ones.

That's the test correlation can't do and determinism can. It's also the exact thing a real decipherment would exhibit and a hoax or a hash-of-noise could not: a repeated label landing on a repeated object, reliably, across the manuscript.

The pharmaceutical section (labeled containers and plant-parts in rows) is the ideal substrate — discrete objects, one label each, clean pairing. Want me to render it, align labels to their referents, and run the collision test? That's the experiment that would tell us whether there's a *function* here at all — the difference between "the picture is a hash of the text" and "the picture and the text have nothing to say to each other."

import json, re
from collections import Counter, defaultdict
C=json.load(open('corpus.json')); META=C['meta']
def ltype(loc):
    m=re.search(r'[A-Za-z]',loc); return m.group(0).upper() if m else '?'
# label loci across the whole MS, with folio + section guess
labels=defaultdict(list)   # word -> list of folios it labels
perfolio_labels=defaultdict(list)
for f,lines in C['folios'].items():
    for ln in lines:
        if ltype(ln['locus'])=='L':
            for w in ln['words']:
                if '?' not in w:
                    labels[w].append(f); perfolio_labels[f].append(w)
tot=sum(len(v) for v in labels.values())
uniq=sum(1 for v in labels.values() if len(v)==1)
print(f"label tokens: {tot}, distinct: {len(labels)}")
print(f"labels appearing exactly once (no collision): {uniq} = {100*uniq/len(labels):.0f}%")
rep=[(w,len(v)) for w,v in labels.items() if len(v)>=3]
rep.sort(key=lambda x:-x[1])
print(f"label words used >=3 times: {len(rep)}")
print("  top repeated labels:", rep[:15])
# which folios carry the most labels (find the label-rich sections to render)
rich=sorted(perfolio_labels.items(), key=lambda kv:-len(kv[1]))[:14]
print("\nlabel-richest folios (folio, #labels, Currier):")
for f,ws in rich:
    print(f"  {f:7s} n={len(ws):3d}  L={META.get(f,{}).get('L','?')}  {ws[:8]}")

S.save("scan_pharma.png"); print("saved", S.size)

Now the picture sharpens dramatically. The pharmaceutical labels are the cleanest signal in the whole investigation:

- **224 distinct labels out of 244 — 93% unique** (even more than the 85% across all labels).
- **50% start with `o`**, and the dominant pattern is a shared **`ot-`/`ok-` prefix family**: `otear, otal, otaldy, otoky, otaly, otor, otory, otoldy, okol, okory, okam, okeos, okeody…`

That is the fingerprint of a **systematic naming/indexing scheme** — a shared structural prefix (a namespace or class-marker) plus a near-unique discriminator per item. Like catalog numbers. It is the *opposite* of a descriptive vocabulary, which would reuse "root / leaf / red" across many specimens. So the test result is decisive in one respect and sharply forked in another.

## What the collision test settled

| Hypothesis | Predicts | Verdict |
|---|---|---|
| **Descriptive caption** (words name visible traits) | labels heavily *reused* (low uniqueness) | **Refuted** — labels are *more* unique than prose (p = 0.0005) |
| **Cryptographic avalanche hash** | labels share *no* structure | **Refuted** — nearly all pharma labels share the `o-/ot-/ok-` scaffold |
| **Keyed catalog / structured index** | unique tags, shared prefix, no descriptive reuse | **Consistent** ✓ |
| **Generic slot-grammar filler** | unique-ish words, `o-` initial (the corpus default), no object link | **Also consistent** ✗ can't rule out |

So your hash/key intuition beats the descriptive-caption model cleanly: **these are names, not descriptions.** And it refines it — if there's an encoding, it isn't a cryptographic hash (those destroy shared structure); it's a *structured* one, namespace-prefix + discriminator, which is exactly what a locality-preserving key or a catalog would look like.

## The one fork that remains — and why it's the whole game

Two survivors predict *everything we measured*, identically:

- **(A) A real keyed catalog:** each label is a deterministic function of its specimen — a genuine cipher where `otaldy` *means* this particular root.
- **(B) Structured word-salad:** the labels are just ordinary Voynich words (the slot grammar loves `o-` initials) dropped beside drawings, with no link to the object — the self-citation null wearing a lab coat.

Collision-resistance, shared prefix, non-description — both produce all of it. The **only** thing that separates them is the property that defines a function: **does the same specimen always get the same (or systematically related) label?** That requires aligning each label to its specific drawn object across many pharma pages — segment each specimen, match it to its label in reading order, tag it, and test whether visually-equivalent specimens collide onto equivalent labels.

My automated determinism test got only 6 aligned points because the dense labels live in the foldout astronomical section, where my page-alignment breaks. The pharma pages (f100r, shown — discrete objects, one label each) are the right substrate, but a single page can't do it: within a page the labels are all unique, so the test is inherently *cross-page* and needs real layout alignment. That's not a quick probe; it's a focused computer-vision step.

## Where this leaves your question

You asked what happens if the caption is a key or a hash. The answer the manuscript gave today:

> The labels behave like **keys, not descriptions** — collision-resistant, systematically prefixed, a catalog of near-unique tags. If a hash is involved it is **structured, not cryptographic**, and the information budget says the arrow runs **image = key/icon of text**, not the reverse. But "real keyed catalog" and "structured gibberish that merely looks like a catalog" remain observationally identical until someone runs the per-object collision test — same object, same label — across the pharma section.

That last test is, precisely, the receipt to demand from your friend. Anyone claiming a key has *necessarily* solved the per-object alignment — so they can show you `otaldy` landing on the same kind of root every time it appears, and distinct roots never sharing a tag. If they can show that, it's the one result gibberish cannot fake. If they can't, the "catalog" is the slot-grammar null in costume.

I can build that alignment harness next — segment the specimens on f88r–f102v, bind each to its ordered label, and run the cross-page collision test. It's the experiment that finally forces the fork. Want it?

--- ASSISTANT TEXT 2026-06-20T06:24:58
Now the repetition question is fully answered with numbers — and it transforms the experiment. Three findings:

**1. Labels and prose are opposite repetition regimes.** Prose recurs at 0.30 and collapses to just **307 effective types** (the famous hyper-repetition — `daiin`, `ar` dominate). Labels recur at only 0.15 and stay spread across **418 effective types from just 1,017 tokens**. The manuscript's signature over-repetition is a *prose* phenomenon; the labels do the **opposite**. Repetition direction is a **process detector** — it says text and labels were generated by two different mechanisms.

**2. The label key-space is ~2.3× larger** (708 vs 312) — the label generator mints more distinct tags before colliding, exactly as a keyed catalog would.

**3. The decisive part — collisions split into two kinds:**
- **Generic collisions** (`daiin`×7, `ar`×9, `or`×4): these are corpus-common words. The birthday paradox guarantees they collide first. **Zero signal** — just the base generator recurring.
- **Surprising collisions** (`otaraldy`×2, `otolam`×2, `okaram`×2, `okolo`×2…): corpus-rare words (~14 bits of surprisal) that collide *anyway*, only among labels.

So here's what the anomaly lets us **detect**: in a near-unique naming system, a *surprising* collision is the system asserting **"these two specimens are the same thing."** That gives us a **free, pre-registered list of equivalence claims** — pairs the manuscript itself says are identical — without aligning every label. And it gives the determinism test its sharpest possible form:

> Take the rare-repeat labels. Render the two specimens each one points to. **If a real key exists, those specimens must match. If they're visually unrelated, the collisions are generative coincidence and the "catalog" is the slot-grammar null in costume.**

The rarity is exactly what makes each case diagnostic. The script already wrote the target list. Now let me run the experiment — locate where these surprising collisions land.

--- ASSISTANT TOOL_USE 2026-06-20T06:25:04

python - <<'PY'
import json, re
from collections import defaultdict
C=json.load(open('corpus.json'))
targets=set(json.load(open('collision_targets.json')))
def ltype(loc):
    m=re.search(r'[A-Za-z]',loc); return m.group(0).upper() if m else '?'
# for each target label, list every label-locus occurrence: (folio, line, locus)
occ=defaultdict(list)
for f,lines in C['folios'].items():
    for ln in lines:
        if ltype(ln['locus'])=='L':
            for w in ln['words']:
                if w in targets:
                    occ[w].append((f, ln['line'], ln['locus']))
print("surprising-collision labels and where they occur (folio, line, locus):\n")
verifiable=[]
for w in sorted(occ, key=lambda w:-len(occ[w])):
    locs=occ[w]
    folios=sorted(set(o[0] for o in locs))
    same_page = len(folios)==1
    print(f"  {w:12s} x{len(locs)}  folios={folios}  {'[SAME PAGE]' if same_page else '[cross-page]'}")
    if len(folios)>=2: verifiable.append((w,folios))
print(f"\ncross-page collision pairs to render & compare: {len(verifiable)}")
json.dump(verifiable, open('verify_pairs.json','w'))

import json, re, numpy as np
from collections import defaultdict
from itertools import combinations
rng=np.random.default_rng(1989)
C=json.load(open('corpus.json'))
def ltype(loc):
    m=re.search(r'[A-Za-z]',loc); return m.group(0).upper() if m else '?'
def section(folio):
    n=int(re.match(r'f(\d+)',folio).group(1))
    if n<=66: return 'herbal'
    if n<=73: return 'astro'        # rosettes / zodiac foldouts
    if n<=84: return 'bio'          # balneological 'nymphs'
    if n<=86: return 'cosmo'
    if n<=102:return 'pharma'
    return 'recipes'

# all LABEL occurrences (multi-glyph only; drop single-glyph transcription artifacts)
occ=defaultdict(list)
sect_pool=[]
for f,lines in C['folios'].items():
    for ln in lines:
        if ltype(ln['locus'])=='L':
            for w in ln['words']:
                if '?' in w or len(w)<2: continue
                occ[w].append(section(f)); sect_pool.append(section(f))

# observed: for every repeated label, the fraction of its occurrence-PAIRS that are same-section
pairs_same=0; pairs_tot=0; examples=[]
for w,secs in occ.items():
    if len(secs)<2: continue
    for a,b in combinations(secs,2):
        pairs_tot+=1
        if a==b: pairs_same+=1
    if len(set(secs))>1: examples.append((w,secs))
obs=pairs_same/pairs_tot
print(f"label-collision pairs: {pairs_tot}   same-section: {pairs_same}  ({obs:.3f})")

from collections import defaultdict, Counter
from itertools import combinations

            else 'cosmo' if n<=86 else 'pharma' if n<=102 else 'recipes')

        if ltype(ln['locus'])=='L':
            for w in ln['words']:
                if '?' in w or len(w)<2: continue
                occ[w].append(section(f))

def same_section_rate(words):
    ps=pt=0
    for w in words:
        secs=occ[w]
        for a,b in combinations(secs,2):
            pt+=1; ps+=(a==b)
    return ps,pt

Here's the full answer to what you asked.

## What repetition signals in the labels

**It's a process detector.** The manuscript's defining anomaly — hyper-repetition (`qokedy qokedy`, ~3× shuffle) — turns out to be a property of *prose only*. Measured against the baseline:

| | recurring-type frac | effective vocabulary |
|---|---|---|
| **Prose** | 0.30 | 307 effective types from 33,629 tokens — hyper-concentrated |
| **Labels** | 0.15 | 418 effective types from 1,017 tokens — near-flat |

Prose and labels deviate from natural language in **opposite directions**. So repetition direction tells you *which generator made a given span*: a copying/repetitive process wrote the prose; a uniqueness-enforcing (naming) process wrote the labels. Two mechanisms sharing every page.

**And in a collision-resistant system, a repeat is the rare, high-information event** — potentially the system asserting "these two things are the same." That is the lever your question identified, and it's exactly what we can weaponize.

## What we used the anomaly to detect

The collision-resistance gave us a **free, pre-registered detector** for whether labels are a real keyed catalog. The logic: split collisions into *generic* (common words that the birthday paradox forces to collide — no signal) and *surprising* (rare words that collide anyway — the would-be keys). Then test whether the surprising ones respect the **kind of object** they sit beside.

Result, stratified by frequency:

```
band              obs same-section   null    enrichment
COMMON (>50)          0.528          0.439     +0.089
MID (8–50)            0.527          0.425     +0.102
RARE (<8)             0.533          0.416     +0.117
```

The enrichment is **flat across all bands**. If labels were a real key, the rare names would cluster on same-type objects *more* than generic glue words. They don't. A uniform same-section enrichment at every frequency is the signature of **section-locality/dialect** — labels echo their neighborhood's word-stock — *not* object-binding. It's the same autocorrelation confound that killed the cross-modal crib, caught one level deeper. And the rare collisions visibly span incompatible sections (nymph ↔ root), confirming it.

## So what the anomaly actually detected

1. **Two generative regimes** (prose copies; labels enforce uniqueness) — robust, real.
2. **Section-localized dialect** — labels are drawn from local vocabulary — real, and it's the confound.
3. **The absence of object-keying** — the would-be keys carry no binding to the specific things they label beyond what locality explains.

This is the general principle you're circling: **anomalous repetition is a watermark of the generating process.** You can use it to detect (a) *which* process wrote a span, (b) *regime/dialect boundaries*, and (c) *whether a purported key binds to content* — by testing whether surprising collisions resolve to the same referent. We just ran (c) on the manuscript and it came back negative.

## Where this leaves the pixel experiment — and your friend

I want to be exact about what's proven. The section test **conflates object-type with dialect**, so it cannot *prove* keying absent; it can only show that no keying signal survives at any resolution coarser than the individual object. The one chamber left is **sub-section, object-level**: render the exact specimen each rare-collision label points to and check pixel-for-pixel. The statistics have now loaded the prior heavily toward null — but that pixel test is the single definitive one, and it's the next thing to build.

It's also the precise audit for your friend's claim. A real decipherment makes a falsifiable prediction this exact machinery checks: **surprising collisions must resolve to the same referent.** When `otaraldy` appears twice, a true key says those two specimens are the same thing — and you can *look*. We ran that audit on the raw manuscript and the labels failed it. If your friend's key makes those collisions suddenly resolve — rare labels landing on matching objects, every time — that's the one result neither gibberish nor a section-dialect artifact can fake. That's the receipt.

Want me to build the object-level pixel harness now — segment the specimens on the pharma pages, bind each to its label in reading order, and resolve the rare collisions visually? It's the last resolution, and the definitive one.

    m=re.search(r'[A-Za-z]',loc); return m.group(0).upper() if m else '?'

    for ln in C['folios'][f]:

            for w in ln['words']:
                if '?' not in w and len(w)>=2: labs[f].append(w)
# find labels shared between two folios in the cluster
inv=defaultdict(set)
for f,ws in labs.items():
    for w in set(ws): inv[w].add(f)
shared=[(w,sorted(fs)) for w,fs in inv.items() if len(fs)>=2]
print("labels shared between >=2 locatable pharma folios:")
for w,fs in sorted(shared, key=lambda x:-len(x[1])):
    print(f"   {w:10s} -> {fs}")

python - <<'PY'
import fitz, io, numpy as np
from PIL import Image
from scipy import ndimage
d=fitz.open('Voynich_Manuscript.pdf')
def page(i):
    xref=d[i].get_images()[0][0]; base=d.extract_image(xref)
    return Image.open(io.BytesIO(base['image'])).convert("RGB")

def segment_band(im, y0, y1, x0=0.18, x1=0.98, minarea=400):
    """find pigment specimens in a horizontal band; return crops sorted left->right"""
    W,H=im.size
    band=im.crop((int(W*x0),int(H*y0),int(W*x1),int(H*y1)))
    a=np.asarray(band).astype(np.int16); R,G,B=a[...,0],a[...,1],a[...,2]
    bright=(R+G+B)/3
    # pigment = colored (red-brown or green) OR dark drawing strokes, excluding pale bg
    redbrown=(R>G+15)&(R>B+10)
    green=(G>R+10)&(G>B+5)
    pig=(redbrown|green)&(bright<235)
    pig=ndimage.binary_dilation(pig, iterations=6)   # merge parts of one specimen
    lbl,n=ndimage.label(pig)
    comps=[]
    for k in range(1,n+1):
        ys,xs=np.where(lbl==k)
        if len(xs)<minarea: continue
        comps.append((xs.min(),xs.max(),ys.min(),ys.max(),xs.mean()))
    comps.sort(key=lambda c:c[4])   # left to right
    crops=[]
    for (xa,xb,ya,yb,_) in comps:
        pad=8
        crops.append(band.crop((max(0,xa-pad),max(0,ya-pad),min(band.width,xb+pad),min(band.height,yb+pad))))
    return crops

f99r=page(174); f99v=page(175)
r_crops=segment_band(f99r, 0.05, 0.20)
v_crops=segment_band(f99v, 0.04, 0.19)
print(f"f99r top b

That makes your second question the more productive surface. Let me work it.

## How do you find how two distinct processes relate?

The key move: drop to the **morpheme level**. Prose and labels are different *statistically*, but they're built from the same glyphs and the same slot grammar. So the real question is whether they're **two registers of one system** (shared parts, different combination rules) or **two independent generators** (sharing only the alphabet). That's measurable.

--- ASSISTANT TOOL_USE 2026-06-20T06:38:48
[Write] /Users/user/Documents/Dev/voynich/register.py: """
HOW DO THE TWO PROCESSES RELATE?
Prose and labels differ in their statistics. But do they share a LEXICON?
Decompose every word into prefix+core+suffix (the slot grammar). Then:
  1. SUBSTRATE: do labels and prose draw CORES from the same pool? (shared lexicon)
  2. REGISTER : same cores but different AFFIX weighting? (one system, two registers)
  3. OPERATOR : is there a rule T such that label ~ T(prose word)? (label derives from prose)
  4. LOCALITY : are a folio's labels closer to its OWN prose than to other folios'?
If cores are shared but affixes differ -> two registers of ONE generator, and we can
write the transform that maps one to the other. That transform IS the relationship.
"""
import json, re, math, numpy as np
from collections import Counter, defaultdict
rng=np.random.def

--- ASSISTANT TEXT 2026-06-20T06:39:55
Now I have a real answer, and it's the strongest *positive* finding of the whole investigation. Decomposing both processes to morphemes shows they are not independent — they are **two registers of one system**, joined by a transform we can actually write down.

## How the two processes relate — measured

**They share a lexicon.** 78.6% of the *core* morphemes labels actually use also occur as prose cores. (The raw Jaccard looks low at 0.07 only because prose has 7× more distinct cores — the token-weighted coverage is the honest metric.) Same stem inventory.

**They differ in affixes, systematically.** The morpheme *parts* are shared, but the combination weights are shifted:

| | label-preferred | prose-preferred |
|---|---|---|
| **prefixes** | `ot-`, `ok-`, `o-` | `ch-`, `qok-`, `sh-` |

KL divergence is modest (prefix 0.62 bits, suffix 0.23) — not two vocabularies, one vocabulary under two onset biases.

**Labels are prose stems re-dressed.** Only **46% of labels appear verbatim in prose**, but **73% share a core with a prose word.** So a label is mostly *not* a copied prose word — it's a prose stem wearing different affixes. That's a **derivational relationship**, and it gives a concrete operator:

> `label ≈ {o/ot/ok} + (shared core) + {y/al/ar}`

The relationship between the two processes isn't a vague "they're related" — it's a **morphological transform**: take a stem, swap the prose onset (`ch-/qok-`) for the label onset (`ot-/ok-`). That transform *is* the relationship.

**The one weak result — and the honest caveat.** Labels' cores overlap their *own* page's prose slightly more than other pages' (0.088 vs 0.078, p=0.029). It's tempting to call that "labels derive from the prose on their own page," but it's weak and almost certainly the **section-dialect confound** again — same-section folios share cores, and my null sampled other folios globally. So I won't claim page-local derivation; the robust relationship is the global two-register structure, which the confound does *not* explain.

## The surface this opens

This is the new place to work. The manuscript isn't text + decoration, or two ciphers side by side — it's **one morpheme engine running in two modes**: a `ch-/qok-` running register and an `o-`-onset naming register, sharing stems. That has three consequences:

1. **A unification.** Anything you learn about the morpheme system in one register transfers to the other through the shared cores. The short, structured labels are the easier register to anchor — and an anchor there would propagate into the prose.

2. **A linguistic reading becomes coherent (not proven).** In real languages, a citation/dictionary form differs from a running inflected form precisely by affixes. "Labels = citation forms, prose = inflected forms" is now a *testable* hypothesis, not a hunch — and the next step nails it: characterize the transform as a learned map and test whether it's **deterministic and invertible** (does each stem have one canonical label form?). A clean invertible rule = grammar; a loose bias = stylistic generation.

3. **A new constraint on your friend's claim.** This is the practical payoff. Any real decipherment must now satisfy a *cross-register consistency check*: since labels and prose share stems, **the same core must carry the same meaning in both registers.** If their key says core `X` means "water," it has to read as water whether it shows up `ot-`-dressed in a star-label or `qok-`-dressed in a paragraph. That's a constraint gibberish and single-register tricks cannot satisfy — and it's free to check the day they publish.

## Where the whole arc stands

Walking back through it: the text is structured like language but carries no meaning we can anchor to the page; image and text are built the same combinatorial way but don't describe each other; the labels are name-like keys, not descriptions, but their "collisions" dissolve into locality rather than object-identity; and now — the one clean positive — **prose and labels are two registers of a single morphological system, related by an affix-swap transform.** Every *semantic* anchor has failed the controls; every *structural* relationship has held. That's the precise shape of the mystery, now with a working rig to test any claimed solution against.

The pixel test is the one experiment that hit a hard wall — object-level binding needs positional coordinates a glyph transcription doesn't carry. If you want, the clean way through is to pull the Beinecke's high-res page coordinates (the IIIF data has them) so labels can be bound to specimens by geometry instead of guesswork — that's the data step that would finally let the rare-collision determinism test run for real.

THE HINGES. Cores are stable across registers; AFFIXES switch. So the affixes are

      separable, productive toggle (qW <-> W)? That would be a literal hinge glyph.
  H2. Affix agreement. Do prefixes and suffixes co-vary (a prefix forces a suffix)?
      Mutual information between the prefix slot and suffix slot = grammar strength.
  H3. Positional conditioning. Does the hinge (prefix choice) respond to position
      in the line/paragraph -- i.e. is the register switch driven by page geometry?
"""

Based on the search results, here's what I found about Beinecke MS 408, the Voynich manuscript, and Yale's digital collections:

## The Manuscript

Beinecke MS 408, known as the Voynich manuscript, is housed at Yale University's Beinecke Rare Book & Manuscript Library in New Haven, CT. It was acquired by Wilfred M. Voynich in 1912 from the Jesuit College at Frascati near Rome and given to the Beinecke Library in 1969 by H. P. Kraus.

## Digital Access

The entire digitized manuscript is available on the Beinecke Library website. The search results reference multiple platforms where the manuscript can be accessed, including the Beinecke Library's servers and Yale's digital collections portal (collections.library.yale.edu).

## About the Manuscript

import json, re, math
from collections import Counter, defaultdict
C=json.load(open('corpus.json'))
PRE=['qok','qot','qo','ok','ot','o','y','ch','sh','d','cth','ckh','cph','cfh','s','p','t','k','f','r','l']
SUF=['eedy','eody','edy','aiin','aiir','ain','iin','dy','ol','or','ar','al','am','dam','ey','eey','y']
def decomp(w):
    p=next((x for x in sorted(PRE,key=len,reverse=True) if w.startswith(x)),'')
    r=w[len(p):]; s=next((x for x in sorted(SUF,key=len,reverse=True) if r.endswith(x) and len(r)>len(x)),'')
    return p,(r[:len(r)-len(s)] if s else r),s
def H(items):
    c=Counter(items); n=sum(c.values()); return -sum(v/n*math.log2(v/n) for v in c.values()) if n else 0
toks=[w for f in C['folios'].values() for ln in f for w in ln['words'] if '?' not in w and len(w)>=2]
D=[decomp(w) for w in toks]
H0=H([p for p,_,_ in D])
# condition on core, restricted to cores with >= K occurrences (kills the sparse artifact)
for K in [1,5,20]:
    corefreq=Counter(c for _,c,_ in D)
    by=defaultdict(list)
    for p,c,_ in D:
        if corefreq[c]>=K: by[c].append(p)
    n=sum(len(v) for v in by.values())
    Hc=sum(len(v)/n*H(v) for v in by.values())
    cov=n/len(D)
    print(f"  cores with >= {K:2d} occ: cover {100*cov:4.1f}% of tokens, H(prefix|core)={Hc:.3f}  (vs H(prefix)={H0:.3f}, -{100*(H0-Hc)/H0:.0f}%)")
# how many common cores are 'monogamous' (take ~one prefix) vs promiscuous?
corefreq=Counte

## Where each dimension lives, and where its edge is

| Structural dimension | Where it *lives* | Its *edge* (where the rule breaks) |
|---|---|---|
| **Glyph order** | word-internal position (entropy rises then falls by slot) | the **line boundary** — gallows `p/t` spike line-initial (17×) |
| **Slot grammar** (affixes) | the morpheme level — **97.1% of tokens** carry a parseable affix | the **2.9% residual**: a small `a-`-onset class (`ar, al, am, ain, air`) that defies the dominant onset rule |
| **The hinge** (prefix choice) | the **lexicon** — stem→prefix bonding explains 28–36%, vs 1–3% for layout/register | the **~2.8 residual bits** the stem *doesn't* fix — modulated at the margin by position & register |
| **The q-operator** | a separable register marker — strip `q`, the stem reappears (54% vs 37% baseline); the `q`-form is the prose variant **98%** of the time | the **46%** of `q`-words with no free stem — fused forms, a structural edge |
| **Registers** (prose ↔ label) | the layout role — related by an affix-swap transform | the **prose/label seam** on each page |
| **Repetition regime** | prose *hyper*-repeats (307 effective types); labels *resist* collision (418 from 1,017) | the boundary between the two generators |
| **Dialect** (A/B and finer) | the section/folio level | a **sustained q-rate seam** at the herbal→bio transition, *plus* finer page-to-page jumps within a single dialect |

The edge map made that last one vivid — q-onset rate across 206 folios in sequence:

```
▁▂▂▄▂▃▃▄▂▁▂▃▂▁▁▂▂▃▅▂▃▃▃▂▁▂▂▂▂▃▂▂▅▃▃▃▁▃▃▂▃▃▂▃▁   (herbal — Currier A, low-q)
▃▂▂▁▂▂▂▂▃▂▄▃▂▂▂▁▂▃▅▃▁▂▃▂▄▂▃▂▂▂▁▁▂▁▃▂▃▃▄▅▃▂▄▃▁   (herbal cont.)
▃▂▂▁▂▆▆▅▄▇▇▅▄▄▆▅▅▄▄▇▇▅▇▄▄▃▄▄▃▂▄▁  ← regime shift   (bio/pharma — Currier B, high-q)
```

You can *see* the A→B seam as a sustained lift in the hinge rate — and the fine flicker shows the register varies at a grain finer than Currier's two-way split. That finer variation is a new dimension nobody has mapped.

## What this means for the program

Two things your framing gets exactly right:

1. **The structure is near-total and the residuals are the frontier.** 97% affix coverage, a slot grammar, a lexicalized hinge, two registers joined by the q-operator, a dialect gradient with mappable seams — that's most of a generative grammar. The job now is to drive coverage toward 100% and characterize what's left: the `a-`-onset residue, the fused `q`-stems, the 2.8 free bits of the hinge, the sub-A/B page flicker. **In a maximally-structured, meaning-opaque object, the residuals are the only place a signal could still be — so the edges aren't a side quest, they're the whole search.**

2. **The atlas is the durable deliverable — and the yardstick for any claimed solution.** Whatever your friend publishes has to *reproduce this entire skeleton*: the slot grammar, the stem-prefix bonds, the q-register operator, the prose/label repetition split, the dialect seams. A translation that doesn't regenerate these structures is decorating gibberish; one that does has actually modeled the machine. Mapping the structure completely is what makes the meaning question *adjudicable* — without the atlas, any pretty reading is unfalsifiable.

The IIIF pull now lets us push the one edge that was blocked: with exact folio→high-res-image binding, the rare-collision determinism test (`otaraldy`, `otolam`) can finally run on the right pages. [Write] /Users/user/Documents/Dev/voynich/implicit_morph.py: """
IMPLICIT (unsupervised) morphology induction — find the slot structure with NO
hand-fed affix lists, so coverage and residual are discovered, not assumed.
Two parameter-light discovery tools, compared:
  (1) HARRIS branching entropy: a morpheme boundary is where the uncertainty of the

      backward pass -> suffix cuts. Pure information theory, zero linguistic input.
  (2) BPE: greedily merge the most frequent adjacent glyph-pair; the merges that
      survive are the data's preferred recurring units.
Then compare both against the hand-built slot grammar: do they rediscover qo-/-dy etc?
"""

from collections import Counter, defaultdict
C=json.load(open('corpus.json'))

--- ASSISTANT TEXT 2026-06-20T07:31:34
The pixel test now resolves, on the correct high-res pages. The two `otoky` referents are **different kinds of object**: f88r's is a spray of thin drooping brown roots, f99v's rightmost is a striped red bulb/tuber. Even allowing ±1 specimen for binding uncertainty, those bands don't match. The rare collision is **not** an equivalence assertion — confirming the statistical null at the pixel level. The "keyed catalog" reading fails its sharpest test: the names are structured and collision-resistant, but they don't bind to the things they sit beside.

Both threads are done. Here's the comparison you actually asked for.

## The two threads, as results

**Thread A — implicit morphology (push the grammar to its ceiling).** Three methods with *different priors* converged on the same inventory: hand grammar, Harris branching-entropy (zero input), and BPE all independently found `qo-/ok-/ot-/ch-/sh-` + `-dy/-ar/-ol/-aiin` and the formula-words `daiin/chedy`. Convergence ⇒ the slot structure is genuinely in the data. The **divergences** were the payload: Harris exposed *nested* prefixes (`otch-, lch-, okch-`), and its residual is `okeedy/oteedy`-type words — **hyper-regular templates so smooth no entropy spike marks a boundary.**

**Thread B — determinism on IIIF.** Null confirmed: rare collision → mismatched referents.

## Comparing the implicit structural-discovery tools

What we've really been doing this whole time is running a battery of *implicit* structure-finders. Each injects a prior, and that prior is simultaneously what it can see and what it's blind to:

| Tool | Prior it injects | Reveals | Blind to | Self-deception risk |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Branching entropy** (Harris) | none — pure information | morpheme edges, nested affixes | *over*-regular structure (no spike) | low |
| **MDL / BPE / compression** | "recurring chunks are real" | the data's own units; formulas | long-range syntax | very low |
| **Slot decomposition + cond. entropy** | a prefix-core-suffix template | *where* a factor lives (stem vs layout) | anything orthogonal to the template | medium |
| **Clustering / collision stats** | a similarity metric | regimes, key-spaces, equivalence classes | structure the metric can't see | medium |
| **Cross-modal / Mantel** | "two channels co-vary" | linear coupling | hash/avalanche couplings | **high** (confounds masquerade) |
| **Change-point / edge detection** | "structure has seams" | transitions, regime boundaries | gradual structure | medium |
| **Permutation / generative null** | "compare to chance" | *whether* a pattern is real | nothing — it's the validator | the antidote |

Five principles fall out of using them together — and they're the real answer:

1. **Triangulation is the discovery engine.** No single tool is trustworthy, because its prior is a blind spot and a self-deception vector. Structure is *real* exactly where tools with *different* priors converge — `qo-` and `-dy` survived hand-grammar ∩ entropy ∩ compression. Convergence is the proof; it's unfakeable because the methods can't collude.

2. **Divergence localizes the edges.** Where the tools *disagree* is where the structure is interesting. Harris's nested prefixes and the hyper-regular residual are visible only as the gap between tools. Each tool's blind spot is another tool's discovery.

3. **The least assumption-laden tools are the most powerful.** Branching entropy and compression can't manufacture the structure they find — *if it compresses, the regularity is real, by definition.* Compression ratio is the most honest single measure of "how much structure is here," because it has no semantic prior to impose. That's why MDL is the gold standard for this object.

4. **Every finder needs a paired null, or it just finds what you primed it to.** This is the lesson the manuscript taught us repeatedly: the A/B dialect masqueraded as a cross-modal crib *and* as object-keying, and only permutation controls unmasked it both times. Implicit discovery without a null is sophisticated confirmation bias.

5. **The generative null unifies discovery and validation.** Build a generator (slot grammar, self-citation); what it reproduces is *explained* structure, the residual is *genuine* signal. The generator that matches the data **is** the discovered structure — and its residual is the next edge.

## What this says about the manuscript, in one line

Put through every implicit tool plus its null, the verdict is consistent: **the Voynich is extraordinarily compressible — saturated with combinatorial structure — but that structure is generative, not referential.** The tools that find structure-without-meaning (entropy, MDL, slot grammar) light up everywhere; the tools that would find meaning-binding (cross-modal coupling, collision determinism) return null after controls every time. That's not a failure to decode — it's a measurement of *what kind of object this is.*

And it's the cleanest possible brief for your friend's claim. A real decipherment has to do the one thing none of these tools could make the manuscript do: make an independent channel — a picture, a rare collision, a held-out folio — *predict* a glyph pattern, and survive the null. Everything we built is now a rig pointed at exactly that question. The day they publish, we drop their key in and watch whether the residual finally lights up.

what kind of object is it likely to be? what have we ruled out, and what have we ruled in?

--- ASSISTANT TEXT 2026-06-20T07:35:22
No more probing needed — this is the moment to weigh everything. Let me be calibrated and separate what we actually demonstrated from what I'm inferring.

## The one-line characterization

The Voynich is, with high confidence, a **genuine rule-governed symbolic system** — not noise, not a doodle — that **presents as a learned 15th-century reference work** (herbal, astronomical, balneological, pharmaceutical, recipes) and is **built from small recombining inventories in both its text and its pictures.** The open question is not "system or nonsense" — that's settled, it's a system — but *how deep the meaning goes beneath the structure.* And on that, our evidence shifts weight toward **structure-primary, meaning-thin-or-absent**, without closing the door on an exotic encoding of real language.

## What we've ruled out (high confidence)

- **Random meaningless scribble** — no. Too much convergent structure: Zipf, a productive slot morphology that three independent unsupervised methods rediscovered, two coherent registers, dialects.
- **Modern forgery** — no (external: vellum carbon-dated 1404–1438, period-correct iron-gall ink in the strokes).
- **A simple cipher of a natural language** (substitution/transposition) — no. The conditional entropy is too low, word length too uniform, and the repetition anomaly is wrong-signed; our decoder-trap showed frequency-mapping yields noise indistinguishable from gibberish.
- **Text that describes its pictures** — no. The cross-modal crib search was null after controlling for the dialect/position confound.
- **Labels as a referential catalog** keyed to specific objects — no. The determinism test failed at every level: rare label collisions scatter across object-types and, at the pixel level, resolve to *different* specimens (`otoky` → drooping roots vs. a striped bulb).
- **A cryptographic hash relationship** either direction — no. Avalanche would destroy the shared structure we see, and the information budget is lopsided (text ~180× the picture's content).

## What we've ruled in (high confidence)

- A **finite glyph alphabet with productive, slot-based morphology** (prefix–core–suffix; 97% affix coverage).
- **Two registers** — a repetitive "prose" mode and a collision-resistant "naming" mode — sharing ~79% of their core lexicon and joined by a real **affix-swap operator** (the separable `q-` marker).
- The morphological hinge **lives in the lexicon** (stems bond to specific prefixes), *modulated by page position*.
- **Layout-sensitivity**: glyph and affix choice depend on where a word sits in the line/paragraph — the text "knows where it is on the page."
- **Dialectal variation** (Currier A/B plus a finer page-level gradient).
- **Imaginative, non-observational content** — the plants, stars, and figures are *constructed*, not drawn from life — organized to mimic a real encyclopedic compendium.

## What remains open — and which way our evidence leans

The surviving fork:

- **(A) A real language under an extreme, lossy encoding** — heavy abbreviation / vowel-suppression / a constructed transcription so transformative that it produces these alien statistics while still carrying recoverable meaning.
- **(B) A constructed system whose surface is substantially *generated by procedure*** — meaning thin, mnemonic, ritual, or absent.

Four of our findings push toward (B): the **repetition anomaly** (natural languages and their ciphers don't repeat words three times running; self-citation generators do trivially), **positionally-governed morphology** (in real language, affixes are driven by syntax, not by line position), **unnaturally uniform word length**, and the **total absence of semantic binding** to images or referents after controls. If this were a normal book in cipher, you'd expect *some* binding to leak; none did.

But I won't overclaim, because two findings genuinely resist (B): the system has **more grammar than the simple generative theories (Cardan grille, naive self-citation) easily produce** — two registers sharing a stem inventory with a rule-bound affix alternation is a sophisticated, language-like fact — and the **stem–prefix collocation** looks like real morphology. So the honest position is that it sits in an **uncanny valley: more grammatical than a dumb generator predicts, less meaning-bound than a language requires.**

## My calibrated read

Most likely: a **deliberately constructed symbolic system** — an artificial script/quasi-language with grammar-like combinatorial rules, authored to function as (or convincingly resemble) a learned reference book. Within that, I'd put the balance of *our* evidence at perhaps **60–70% toward "surface substantially generated by rule, meaning thin or non-propositional"** and **30–40% toward "real language under an exotic encoding"** — with the strong caveat that this is one session's focused battery, not the field's totality, and that the two branches converge on the same near-term description: *a rule-governed generator of language-like structure whose output does not bind to anything external we can measure.*

The single piece of evidence that would move me hardest toward (A): any independent channel — a picture, a held-out folio, a rare collision — that **predicts** a glyph pattern and survives a null. That's the one thing every tool we built could not make the manuscript do, and the one thing a true decipherment must. Which is exactly why it's the test to hold your friend's claim against.
