## Crack #1 — Syntax: there essentially isn't any

The biggest text gap, now measured. Conditioning on the previous word drops entropy a lot (10.37 → 4.23 bits) — but a **within-line shuffle gives the identical 4.23.** Order-specific information is **+0.00 bits.** Cross-word affix binding is a faint 0.125 bits.

That means: words are predictable from *which line they're in* (local clustering), but their **sequence carries no information** — it's a *positional bag of words*, not a grammar. Real language has several bits of word-order information; this has none. This is a strong structural fact and it pushes the **prose** further toward the generative/procedural end: the prose has rich morphology but no syntax. (Caveat: this is *local* adjacency; long-range topical organization — consistent with our section-dialect findings — may still exist. The absence is of sentence-grammar, not of all order.)

## Crack #2 — The calendar: real counting that binds to the world

The zodiac medallions carry **~30 labels each** (f72v1: 30, f73v: 31, f72r2/v2: 32, f73r: 29…) — one per nymph, arranged in concentric rings around a central beast. **~30 = days of a month**, twelve signs = the year. And these are the folios that carry **month-names in plain script.**

This matters out of proportion to its size: it's the **first structure we've found that demonstrably binds to an external referent.** The herbal text didn't describe its plants; the labels didn't key to their objects; but the zodiac section *counts days* and *names months in readable script.* Part of the manuscript is genuinely *about something real.*

## What this does to the picture

It splits it — and that's the honest update. The manuscript is **heterogeneous**:

- The **prose** (herbal, recipes) looks increasingly *generative*: rich morphology, no syntax, no semantic binding. The recipes even open entries with a tiny templatic set (`daiin/saiin/sain/dain`) like rubber-stamped "Item—" markers.
- The **astronomical/calendar** section shows *referential intent*: it counts real days, organizes by real months, and labels them in real script.

So the likeliest object isn't uniform. It's a constructed compendium where **at least one section has genuine real-world structure (the calendar)** while the bulk of the running text behaves like a rule-governed generator with no recoverable syntax. That raises an interesting possibility we couldn't see before: the author *intended* meaning throughout (the calendar proves intent), but the generative prose either encodes it in a way that leaves no syntactic trace, or simulates the meaning the calendar genuinely has.

The richest unexamined surfaces from here are the ones with **external anchors** — the rosettes map, the balneological flow-network, the color channel, and above all the **plain-script marginalia**, because anything that binds to the readable world is where a real decoding can get its first foothold. Want me to go after the marginalia and the calendar's month-names next — the one place the manuscript already speaks in a script we can read?

My thread is done. The manifest hypothesis gets **real but modest support** — and the answer to "how would we find the boundaries" is: vocabulary change-points, validated three ways.

## Treating the text as inventory — what the boundaries show

Sliding a vocabulary-dissimilarity detector (TextTiling on morpheme bags) along all 4,116 prose lines found **308 candidate manifest boundaries** (~13 lines apart). Three validations:

1. **Segments are more internally coherent than random cuts** — 0.686 vs 0.668, p = 0.002. Real, but the effect is *small*: the inventories aren't sharply separated.
2. **Boundaries align with physical paragraph breaks at 42% vs 23% chance** — so paragraphs *are* partial manifest units, but the *majority* of vocabulary shifts (58%) fall **between** paragraphs. That's exactly the "if they broke that way" case: there's manifest structure that doesn't respect the visible paragraphing.
3. **New item-types spike right after a boundary** (0.294 vs 0.249) — each segment opens a fresh batch of items, the signature of a new inventory beginning.

But the **manifest signatures are not distinct identities** — every segment's top morphemes are the same shared stock (`y, d, ch, sh, ol, aiin`) in shifting proportions.

**The honest read:** the text behaves like **overlapping inventories drawn from one shared item-pool**, with soft boundaries — not a series of crisply-separated distinct manifests. The manifest unit is roughly paragraph-scale (~13–16 lines), boundaries are fuzzy, and the "items" are recombined from a common inventory rather than each manifest having its own closed vocabulary. This fits everything else: a positional bag with weak large-scale organization, where the strongest real segmentation remains the coarse section/dialect seams, and finer manifest boundaries are present but gradient, not crisp.

So your inventory reframing *works as a lens* — the text does break into item-lists with detectable, partly-hidden boundaries — but it reveals the manuscript as a **continuous re-drawing from a shared pool of items**, which is a very generative-process-looking thing to be.

# RAW FINDINGS — Color/Pigment as Code vs Decoration in the Voynich Manuscript

## TASK 1 — PALETTE SIZE (15 pages, sections herbal/astro/balneo/pharma/recipe)

Sampled saturated pixels (S&gt;0.30, excluding vellum + ink); 45,000 pigment pixels clustered. Pigment covers only ~2–14% of any page (most pages 5–10%). The palette is TINY — 3 working pigments plus 2 minor accents:

| Pigment family | Hue (deg) | Median RGB | Usage fraction (of pigment pixels) |
|---|---|---|---|
| Red-brown / ochre | 18–50 | (134,111,84) | **0.85** (inflated — overlaps aged parchment/ink) |
| Red (rust) | 0–18 | (141,80,63) | 0.07 |
| Blue | 175–260 | (57,72,94) | 0.04 (heavily faded toward gray) |
| Green | 75–170 | (83,123,96) | 0.04 |
| Yellow | 50–75 | (116,113,75) | 0.01 (trace) |

KMeans (k=4–8) refuses to find more than these families — extra clusters just split red-brown into brightness shades (V from 0.26→0.73), i.e. one pigment at different dilutions, not new pigments. **Effective palette ≈ 3 colors (green, red-brown, faded blue) + trace yellow/red.** Cluster centers above. This confirms the "tiny palette" hypothesis.

## TASK 2a — SECTION DEPENDENCE (page_features.json, 209 pages, + HSV recompute)

Green is strongly section-dependent; blue is uniformly trace everywhere:

| Section | green | red-brown | blue | dominant |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| herbal | 0.060 | 0.106 | 0.002 | red-brown + high green |
| astro/zodiac | **0.007** | 0.081 | 0.002 | red-brown (almost no green) |
| balneo | 0.036 | 0.096 | 0.002 | red-brown + green |
| pharma | 0.057 | 0.105 | 0.002 | red-brown + green |
| recipes/stars | **0.000** | 0.103 | 0.000 | red-brown only |

Pattern is fully explained by SUBJECT MATTER: green tracks foliage (herbal/pharma/balneo pools high; astro &amp; text-heavy recipes ~zero). Red-brown (roots, ink, tubes, parchment) is roughly constant. Blue is rare everywhere; 64% of what little blue exists is in the herbal section (flower accents). No section has a distinctive pigment that isn't predicted by what it depicts.

## TASK 2b — RULE-BASED vs AD HOC (direct image inspection)

- **Herbal (f49r / raw_119):** leaves GREEN, root RED-BROWN, tiny BLUE flower dabs. The leaf/root/flower color assignment is the same organ→color rule seen across herbal pages — decorative botanical convention.
- **Balneo (bal_75r, bal_78r):** pools and channel water are GREEN, not blue; pipes/tubes are red-brown ochre with occasional blue connector. Water-is-green is applied consistently but is the OPPOSITE of a naturalistic blue-water code — a fixed stylistic habit, and the same green used for foliage (no dedicated "water" pigment).
- **Astro/zodiac (f73 / pf_73r):** the strongest single piece of evidence. A radial chart with ~30 stars and dozens of nymphs — the ideal substrate for a color code — is **almost entirely uncolored**: stars are bare ink outlines, nymphs unpainted, only the central Taurus bull gets a thin green wash. Stars are NOT color-differentiated. If pigment carried information, this is where it would appear; it doesn't.

## TASK 2c — COLOR vs TEXT STATISTICS

- corr(pigment, text-likeness/edge) = **−0.43**; corr(pigment, ink coverage) = +0.38. Pigment simply marks "drawing pages" vs "text pages" — no finer alignment.
- High-pigment pages mean edge 51 vs low-pigment 68 (just drawings vs prose).
- Label-fraction by section: astro **0.25** (most labels) vs herbal/balneo 0.02, recipes 0.00. Critically, the astro section has the MOST labels but the LEAST pigment (green 0.007). A color-coding channel would put color where the dense labels/symbols are; instead color and label-density are anti-correlated.

## TASK 3 — VERDICT: **DECORATION, not a code.**

Reasoning (all quantitative tests point the same way):
1. **Palette too small to carry much information** — effectively 3 distinguishable pigments (green / red-brown / faded blue), each ≤~5% coverage. Extra k-means clusters are dilution shades of one ink, not new symbols. Channel capacity ≈ &lt;2 bits per painted region.
2. **Pigment is predictable from the depicted object** — leaves→green, roots/tubes→red-brown, water→green, flowers→blue dabs. This is the decisive decorative test: color is a function of *what is drawn*, with no residual variation that could carry side information.
3. **Color is absent exactly where a code would be densest** — the labeled radial star/zodiac charts (f73) are nearly colorless; label-fraction and pigment are anti-correlated (astro 25% labels / 0.007 green).
4. **No naturalistic or systematic signaling** — water painted green (not blue), stars left uncolored, application is loose/sloppy washes with brightness varying by dilution, not by category.

## UNCERTAINTY / CAVEATS
- Blue is badly faded; my masks under-count it (it reads as bluish-gray), so blue's true usage may be somewhat higher than 4% but is still clearly minor — does not change the verdict.
- The red-brown 0.85 fraction is inflated by overlap with aged parchment/ink in hue space; the genuine red-brown pigment fraction is lower, but it is unambiguously the dominant pigment.
- Analysis is at the page/region level; I did not test whether color *retouching by a later hand* introduced noise (a known Voynich issue) — but later, inconsistent retouching, if present, would further argue AGAINST a deliberate coding channel.
- A subtle code in a *subset* of regions (e.g., specific nymph hair/skin tones) cannot be fully excluded at this sampling resolution, but the primary structural tests (palette size, object-predictability, absence in label-dense charts) consistently indicate decoration.

# RAW FINDINGS: Voynich "Rosettes" foldout (f85v–f86r)

## Source / image notes
- Foldout is split across IIIF canvases in `iiif_canvases.json`. The full rosettes diagram is on the **verso side**, canvas labeled **"85v and 86r (foldout)"**, service `https://collections.library.yale.edu/iiif/2/1006231`, **7925×7268 px**. (The recto-side canvases 1006228/1006229/1006230 are the back/other side of the same physical foldout.)
- All observations below are from that canvas. The vellum has prominent fold creases (a 3×3 grid of folds), some pigment offset/ghosting, and the left edge disappears into the binding gutter.

## Overall layout: 3×3 grid of 9 rosettes
The nine large circular "rosettes" are arranged in an approximate **3×3 grid**. Using compass-style labels (TL/TC/TR, ML/C/MR, BL/BC/BR):

- **TL (NW)** — Large double-ring rosette with an **empty oval/eye-shaped void at center** (vesica-like blank oval). Marginal text down its left side (page-margin text, mirror-readable). A bundled-tube/pipe structure exits toward the center.
- **TC (N)** — **Sun/star rosette**: bold blue-and-gold radiating "sun rays" (a many-pointed star burst) in the center, ringed by scalloped petals. One of the most sun-like features.
- **TR (NE)** — **Star-field rosette**: center packed with clearly drawn **stars** (not mere dots), wrapped by spiral text; surrounded by overlapping **cloud-band / scalloped petals**. The **"castle"** structure sits on its upper-right margin (see below).
- **ML (W)** — Radial **petal/leaf-burst** (overlapping leaf or feather petals) around a gold star-burst hub; ring of text labels around the rim.
- **C (CENTER)** — The **hub**. Multiple thick **concentric rings** of "scale/feather" texture with **text between the rings**; the innermost field contains several **vessel/turret/tower-like structures** (small architectural or container forms) over a blue ground. Connects outward in multiple directions (the dominant node).
- **MR (E)** — Center densely filled with **hundreds of small blue/green circles ("bubbles"/cells/scales)** in a radial pattern with a budding/branching central form. Causeways exit top and bottom.
- **BL (SW)** — Complex cluster of forms; notable **small medallion circle in the extreme SW corner** containing a "T"/cross-like mark with surrounding text (the often-cited corner roundel).
- **BC (S)** — **Six-pointed blue star/flower** (six bold blue petals) over a gold star-field, ringed by a band of text labels; small central hub.
- **BR (SE)** — Architectural rosette: a **building/wall with a colonnade or arched gallery** (rectangular structure with vertical bars), a **blue cross/star (X) marking**, star-fields, dense text; a **radiating sun-like motif in the extreme SE corner**.

## Causeways / connectivity
Rosettes are joined by **"causeways"**: thick bands rendered as scaled/feathered/wavy tubes, plus in places **bundled pipes ending in circular "mouths"** (a cluster of ~5–6 dark-rimmed circular tube-ends is clearly visible on the NW→C diagonal). The central rosette's rim also shows a band of round tube-ends (like round wall-towers / pipe openings).

Observed connection pattern (a "king's-graph"-like lattice — orthogonal neighbors **and** diagonals to center):

```
TL --- TC --- TR
 | \    |    / |
ML --- C  --- MR
 | /    |    \ |
BL --- BC --- BR
```

Edge list (each = at least one causeway band; honest confidence noted):
- TL–TC (top edge band)            — fairly clear
- TC–TR (top edge bands)           — clear (two/wavy bands along top)
- TR–MR (right edge, vertical tube)— clear
- MR–BR (right edge, vertical tube)— clear
- BR–BC (bottom edge)              — clear
- BC–BL (bottom edge)              — fairly clear
- BL–ML (left edge)                — likely (gutter obscures)
- ML–TL (left edge)                — likely (gutter obscures)
- C–TL (NW diagonal)               — clear (the bundled "pipe-mouth" cluster)
- C–TC (up)                        — likely
- C–TR (NE diagonal)               — likely
- C–MR / C–ML / C–BC etc.          — center clearly radiates causeways in multiple/all directions; exact count uncertain

So: **8 perimeter rosettes form a ring of edge-to-edge causeways; the central rosette is a hub linked outward (at least to the four corners, plausibly to all 8).** The center is unambiguously the most-connected node. Exact causeway *counts* per edge are uncertain because bands split/merge and fold-creases interrupt them; most edges show 1–2 bands.

## Directional / map-like vs cosmological features
Evidence is genuinely **mixed** — this is the most "map-like" diagram in the manuscript but not decisively a geographic map:

Map / geographic cues:
- The **"castle"**: a walled enclosure with **crenellated battlements** (a clear row of merlon "teeth") and a tower, on the TR rosette's upper margin. Frequently read as **swallowtail/Ghibelline merlons → N. Italian fortification** (dating/locale clue). Visible but the merlon shape (swallowtail vs square) is hard to confirm at this resolution.
- A **second architectural structure** in BR (colonnade/arched gallery + walls).
- **Causeways with pipe-mouths / round-towered walls** resembling roads, aqueducts, or fortified connections between settlements.
- Overall **island-and-isthmus** feel: nine "places" linked by paths.

Cosmological / abstract cues:
- **TC sun-burst** and **BR/SE corner sun** (radiating motifs); **TR and BC star-fields**; the **TL central void/oval**.
- Heavy **concentric-ring** construction (center) typical of cosmological/zodiacal wheels elsewhere in the manuscript.
- **No unambiguous T-O map element**, no clear cardinal-direction labels, no coastline/landmass outlines, no recognizable place-glyphs. The SW corner roundel has a "T"-like mark but it's a small device, not a T-O world diagram.

Assessment: best described as a **schematic / cosmographic "map of places or cosmos"** — a stylized diagram of nine interconnected realms/zones rather than a literal topographic map. The castle + causeways push it toward **map/itinerary**; the suns, stars, and concentric rings push it toward **cosmological/cosmographic**. Most defensible label: a **cosmographic schematic with map-like (itinerary/place-network) structure**. Real uncertainty remains; no element forces a purely geographic reading.

## Text placement and amount
Text is **very dense and pervasive** — this is one of the most text-heavy diagrams in the manuscript. Distribution:
- **Concentric rings**: long lines of script running *between* the rings (esp. the central rosette — several full circular text rings).
- **Radial spokes / petal labels**: short word-labels at the base of each petal/ray (BC and ML especially — a label per petal around the rim).
- **Along causeways**: text follows the curving tube bands between rosettes.
- **Spiral labels**: e.g., spiral text wrapping the TR star field.
- **Page-margin text**: a block in the TL margin (left edge), readable as normal page text.
- **Tiny labels** scattered around the castle, the corner roundels, and structures.
Rough estimate: **several hundred words/tokens** total across the foldout — far more text than a typical single Voynich illustration.

## Caveats
- Left column (ML/BL and their causeways) is partly lost in the binding gutter; those edges are inferred.
- Causeway *multiplicities* and the full set of center→perimeter links are not all individually confirmable due to fold damage and overlapping bands.
- "Castle/merlon," "sun," "star," "pipe," "tower," "vessel" are interpretive labels for ambiguous drawings; I report what the marks resemble, not confirmed identities.</result>

## TOPOLOGY (graph abstraction)
Recurring primitives, in order of frequency:
1. **Linear chain / cascade (most common, ~8 folios: 75r,78r,79r,79v,80r,83r,84r,84v):** source (tube/fountain/nymph-with-vessel) → pool → conduit → pool, oriented top→bottom on the page. Flow direction is explicitly implied by gravity (vertical drop tubes), drip/spray marks (77r), and nozzle terminals. **Source→sink directionality is the dominant organizing principle.** Trees and cycles are essentially ABSENT — I found no closed loop (no cycle) and no true branching tree; the closest to branching is the manifold.
2. **Distribution manifold (75v, 77r, and the feed-pipe on 78r):** ONE horizontal supply pipe → a rank of regularly-spaced outlets/taps, each individually LABELED, each over one nymph/station. This is a 1-to-N fan-out (star from a bus), the only "branching" topology present. ~6 taps (77r), ~10 stations (75v).
3. **Twin/paired pool (83v):** two adjacent basins fed symmetrically from opposite sides — the one bilaterally-symmetric layout.
4. **Reservoir pair (84v):** two big circular holding-pools joined by a single long pipe — a 2-node chain at large scale (visually a miniature of the f86 "rosettes").
- Tube fittings are drawn as plumbing hardware: ribbed barrel-couplings/valves (78r), scalloped/ruffled pipe edges with internal flow-lines (75v), decorated spray-nozzle terminals (78r ends), drip marks at tap outlets (77r). Strongly supports a literal plumbing/flow reading over decorative.

## QUANTITATIVE REGULARITIES
- **Pool count per illustrated folio: tightly clustered at 1–3** (mean ≈2.0 over the 16 illustrated folios; max 3; never &gt;3). Very regular.
- **Tubes per folio: 1–3** (mode 2). tubes ≈ pools roughly (typically pools−1 or pools, consistent with chain topology where N pools need ~N−1 connecting tubes plus a source feed).
- **Nymphs/pool ratio does NOT converge to a constant.** Range 1.7–13.5, mean ≈5.6, median ≈4.85. Two regimes: "row/manifold + cascade" pages run high (78r 9.5, 78v 9.0, 82r 9.0, 84v 13.5), while "linked discrete basins" pages run low (77v 1.7, 83v 2.0, 83r 3.0). So nymph-count tracks pool TYPE, not a fixed quota.
- **Nymphs vs pools:** the only notable correlation, r≈0.47, p≈0.05 (weak-positive; bigger/more pools hold more nymphs, expected).

## TEXT ↔ DIAGRAM CORRELATIONS (all WEAK / NULL)
- pools vs label-words: r=0.08 (none). tubes vs label-words: r=0.15 (none). nymphs vs label-words: r=0.06 (none).
- nymphs vs total words: r=−0.19 (ns). pools vs total words: r=−0.35 (ns, p=0.15). tubes vs total words: r=−0.39 (ns, p=0.11).
- **No evidence that tube/pool/nymph counts are encoded by word-count or label-count.** The slight NEGATIVE trend (more diagram → fewer total words) is just the trade-off between drawing space and text space on a page; not a content relationship.
- Label loci (`L`) presence DOES track the manifold motif qualitatively: the pages with the most label-words (82r/v=15, 84r=15, 80r=10, 77r=10, 75v=37) are exactly the labeled-row/manifold/linked-basin pages — i.e., labels are attached to individually-distinguished outlet stations, NOT to pools or tubes in bulk. 75v is the extreme (37 label-words over its rank of stations). Text-only/pure-cascade pages (75r, 78v, 79r/v, 80v, 84v) have 0 label loci.

## KEY TAKEAWAYS
1. The section is NOT uniform: it alternates illustrated plumbing pages with text-heavy interludes (76r fully text; 76v, 80v, 82v mostly text).
2. Dominant topology = **directed linear cascade, top→bottom, gravity-fed**; the only fan-out is the **labeled distribution manifold**; **no cycles, no real trees**.
3. **Pools are quantized to 1–3 per page; tubes 1–3 (mode 2)**; these are the most stable numerical regularities.
4. **Nymph-per-pool is NOT constant** (1.7–13.5) — it encodes pool type, not a fixed count.
5. **Labels attach to outlet/station nodes of manifolds**, and label-rich pages = manifold pages; but **no quantitative law links figure/tube/pool counts to word or label counts** (all |r|&lt;0.4, all ns).

--- ASSISTANT TEXT 2026-06-20T07:55:20
That's a sharper frame than mine, and it fits the evidence better. A **manifest** is a static snapshot — a list of what's present. A **transition log** is a record of *change*: the same ingredients persist, but which one leads (**priority**) and how much of each (**proportion**) shifts from entry to entry. That single distinction explains things "manifest" couldn't:

- **Why the vocabulary is shared everywhere** (78.6% shared cores) — same ingredients throughout.
- **Why prose hyper-repeats adjacently** (`qokedy qokedy qokedy`) — you re-log a persistent item as its state changes.
- **Why segmentation was weak/overlapping** — a process changes *gradually*, so boundaries are soft.
- **Why the balneological network is a directed gravity-fed cascade** — that's literally a transition/flow diagram, not an inventory.

And it makes a **different, testable prediction** than manifest. A manifest is *exchangeable* — reorder the entries, nothing is lost. A transition log is a *trajectory* — entry order carries process information even with no grammar. We already proved word order *within* a line is uninformative; but I never tested whether the **line/entry sequence** is a directional process. That's the discriminator. Let me run it.

--- ASSISTANT TEXT 2026-06-20T08:01:08
Now I can compare both of your framings against the data directly.

## Transition-log vs roster — what held, what didn't

**Transition-log (process over time):** *partial.* Order carries information (adjacent lines more similar than distant, p=0.0025; entries open `q-/qot-/-dy`, close `-y/-iin`), but there's **no directional trajectory** (T2 null) — it isn't a process moving from A to B.

**Roster / org-chart (population of entities with attributes):** *the better fit for the structure.* The predictions it makes that are distinct from a bag all came back positive:

- **A — Persistent membership with turnover.** Adjacent paragraphs share **0.18** of their entity (core) set vs **0.14** shuffled, decaying with distance. There's a real, slowly-turning-over membership — roster-like continuity.
- **C — Entities wear changing attributes.** Each persistent core appears in **~16 distinct affix-dressings** — the same entity tagged many ways. Exactly "same ingredient, different attribute."
- **D — Layered attributes (hierarchy).** **21% of words carry stacked prefixes** (2–3 layers) — a nested attribute structure, the org-chart signature.

But two predictions *failed*, and they're the telling ones:

- **B — the persistent "members" are generic glue** (`a, e, ol, y, ch`), present in ~80% of paragraphs, while **72% of cores are one-offs.** A real roster has *distinctive* persistent members (named students); here the stable layer is featureless and the distinctive layer churns completely.
- **C (drift) — attributes don't track position.** An entity's `q`-status shows ~zero correlation with where it sits in the document (mean r = +0.01). So attributes *vary*, but not *over time* — there's no longitudinal "status changing across the record."

## What this resolves

"Same ingredients, different priority and proportion" is **confirmed for priority and proportion** (position-template + 16 attribute-dressings per entity + hierarchy) but **denied for time** (no trajectory, no attribute drift). So it's not a *log* of changes-over-time, and not a *roster* of stable named members. It's the structural intersection of both with the temporal axis removed:

> **A combinatorial registry** — each entry draws a persistent-but-churning population of entities from one shared pool, dresses each entity in layered, varying attribute-markers, and orders them by position and hierarchy rather than by syntax or chronology.

That high churn (72% one-offs) + local continuity + layered attributes + no temporal drift is the same shape every channel has shown: the **manifold** (one source → N tagged stations), the **rosette grid** (9 hubs, hierarchical), the **calendar** (30 tagged days), the **morphology** (stems + stacked affixes). The book keeps building *labeled-entities-in-a-hierarchy, recombined positionally* — and pointedly **not** a sequence that goes anywhere.

Which is why the temporal/process readings keep coming back weak: the manuscript is **organized like a catalog of structured entities, not narrated like a record of events.** Your instinct was right that it's "ingredients with shifting priority/proportion" — it just lacks the time axis that would make it a log.

The data answers you directly — and it's the inverse of what I guessed, which makes it sharper. **The surface is the line.** But the glue doesn't mark the edges; the edges are *reserved for specialized markers*, and the glue fills the interior:

glue fraction by position:  first 15%   second 31%   interior 28%   penult 25%   last 18%

- **line-openers:** `daiin, saiin, dain, sor, sol, sain`
- **line-closers:** `daiin, dy, dam, am, dal, ol`

## The answer to your question

You're right that glue implies a surface — and the surface isn't grammar, it's **the line on the page.** The "glue" isn't binding words into sentences (there are none); it's **structuring the line**: marked openers, marked closers, and filler in between, with lines filling to a semi-regular length (CV ≈ 0.31 — adhering to the margin like ruling). The organizing rules operate on *where a word sits in the 2-D line*, not on *what precedes it grammatically*.

So the most important reframe of the whole investigation: **"no syntax" doesn't mean "no structure" — it means the syntax is spatial.** There's no *sentence* grammar; there's a strong *line* grammar — a layout grammar. The text is generated to fill a visual template, and a quarter of all tokens are scaffolding adhering to that template.

And the single most consequential consequence: **`daiin` — the most common "word" in the manuscript (2.2% of everything) — is best read not as a lexeme but as a line-boundary marker.** It dominates *both* the opener and closer slots. It's punctuation, a bracket, a line-delimiter — structure, not content. A large fraction of what looked like "vocabulary" is actually the connective tissue of the page-grid.

## Why this matters for everything before it

This is *why* every meaning-test came back null. We kept probing for lexical/sentence meaning, but the dominant organizing principle is **layout** — the same principle that puts labels beside drawings, days around a wheel, stations along a manifold, rosettes in a grid. The manuscript's deep grammar is *the grammar of the page*: open the line, fill it from the shared pool, close it, move down. If meaning lives anywhere, it's in the ~75% interior "content" words — organized as **positional bags inside a line-template**, i.e. a registry laid out on a surface, not prose.

So your instinct closes a loop: the glue proved there's a surface; the surface turned out to be the line itself; and that recasts the whole object as **text engineered to fill a spatial form** rather than to say sentences.

One thread still owed: the **marginalia agent hasn't reported.** It's the only channel in plain script — the one place we could check whether the interior "content" words name real things or just fill the form. I'll integrate it the moment it lands; given everything above, it's now the hinge between "registry of real entries" and "form filled with structured nothing."

Layout-primary notation is a real family, and music is a smart candidate to test. Let me first place it among the alternatives, then settle it empirically.

**Structures whose meaning lives in layout, not sequence:** music notation (pitch=vertical, time=horizontal), **tables/ledgers/registries** (rows×fields), **calendars/ephemerides** (time-grids), **liturgical/recitation texts** (litanies, psalmody — formulaic, performance-structured), recipes/formularies, weaving/knitting grids, maps/diagrams.

Music is **ruled out** — and the test is decisive on exactly the property that separates music from a table:

| Music requires | Voynich has |
|---|---|
| Repeated phrases (motifs) | 3-grams recur at 1% (vs 0.2% chance); **4-grams: 0%** |
| Refrains (repeated lines) | **0 repeated lines, anywhere** |
| Meter (periodicity) | word-length autocorrelation **0.06** — no beat |
| Deep melodic memory | glyph memory drops only 0.36 bits — shallow |

Music is the most sequential signal there is — motifs, refrains, meter, a melody you can hum forward. The Voynich has **no motifs beyond 3 words, not a single repeated line, and no meter.** The one music-like feature — line-final cadences (`daiin/dy/am` closing 11% of lines) — is equally explained by **table rows ending consistently**. So the line-structure that *felt* musical is row-structure, not phrase-structure. Among layout systems, the evidence points hard at **table / registry / list** (rows with marked edges, positional fill, no sequence) and away from music, which needs the sequence we proved isn't there.

## But the marginalia agent just changed the picture — this is the headline

The last agent came back, and it's the most important result we've gotten. The plain-script marginalia are **legible**, and they're a clean **Occitan / southern-French Romance** calendar:

> **mars** (Mar), **abril** (Apr), **may** (May), **yony** (Jun), **yoller** (Jul), **augst** (Aug), **octembre** (Oct), **novembre** (Nov)

— each written next to the matching zodiac sign **in correct order** (ram→April, bull→May, crab→July…). Plus f116v's debased Latin (`maria, vix, valden, "michiton oladabas"`) and a possibly-German `den mus der` on f66r.

Three things fall out, and they're decisive:

1. **The images are genuinely referential.** A 15th-century reader *recognized the zodiac pictures* — they're conventional, correctly sequenced signs — and labeled them. The picture-layer carries real, conventional meaning. This independently confirms what the calendar-count (~30/wheel) suggested.

2. **The text was opaque even to contemporaries.** Crucially, the annotator wrote months in *their own Romance language* — they did **not** translate any Voynichese, they labeled the *pictures*. Someone literate, near the time of creation, **could not read the cipher-text either** and fell back on the recognizable drawings. Our null results aren't a modern failure; the text was unreadable from the start.

3. **A real cultural anchor.** Occitan/southern-French/Catalan month-names place a handler in the **Toulouse–Lombardy arc** — which dovetails with the rosettes' swallowtail-merlon "castle" (North Italian fortification). The object lived in that southern-French/North-Italian sphere.

## Where this leaves the whole picture

The marginalia confirms the **split** we kept finding, and confirms it from *outside* our statistics:

- **Image-layer: real, referential, conventional** — a recognizable zodiac/calendar, an organized herbal, a cosmographic map, anchored to a real time and place.
- **Text-layer: opaque, syntax-free, layout-organized** — a table/registry filling a line-grid, generative-looking, giving no crib even to a contemporary who held it.

What it does **not** do is hand us a translation: the readable script is all *marginal annotation of the pictures*, never a key to the cipher. So the central fork stays open — the text is still either an extremely obscure encoding or a structured construction — but everything around it is now firmly real: real pictures, real calendar, real place, real 15th-century reader who was just as stuck as we are.

That's all four agents in, music tested, and the marginalia decoded as far as it goes. The honest end-state: **a real southern-European reference book whose illustrations mean what they appear to mean, wrapped around a text laid out like a registry that has resisted reading since the century it was made.**

--- ASSISTANT TEXT 2026-06-20T16:11:02
This builds the registry into an actual **schema**. The columns are real, and they're *inside the word* — each word is a 3-field record.

## The columns: `prefix | core | suffix` = `attribute | entity | attribute`

| Field | Distinct values | Top-10 coverage | Entropy | Type |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **prefix** | 15 | 94% | 3.3 bits | **closed enum** (attribute) |
| **core** | 3,042 | 48% | 6.8 bits | **open key** (entity) |
| **suffix** | 18 | 90% | 3.3 bits | **closed enum** (attribute) |

That is exactly a database record: two small controlled-vocabulary attribute fields flanking one large open identifier. And the **dependency structure clinches it**:

- `I(prefix ; suffix) = 0.20 bits` — the two attributes are **independent of each other**.
- `I(prefix ; core) = 1.33`, `I(core ; suffix) = 1.61` — but **both depend on the entity**.

Entity → attribute₁, entity → attribute₂, attribute₁ ⊥ attribute₂. That is the precise signature of a table where the key constrains each field but the fields don't constrain each other. **Each word is a row in a 3-column table; each line is an unordered row of such records; the manuscript is a table of ~38,000 records.** The "columns" aren't positions across the line (that template was weak) — they're the morphological slots.

## Further invariants from layout position (beyond `daiin`)

The line ("row") has a consistent **format** — every feature shifts predictably with position:

```
pos    word-len   gallows%   q-prefix%   has-prefix%
1st      5.54        52         13           64      ← long, often BARE-stem
2–3      5.2         ~52        ~17          ~83     ← fully dressed
mid      5.24        56         18           81      ← dressed, gallows-rich
last     4.89        43          9           73      ← short, plain, q-stripped
```

So beyond the `daiin` delimiter there are at least **five new positional invariants**:

1. **The opener is a barer key** — line-initial words are *longer* but carry a prefix only 64% of the time vs 81–83% interior. The row often *leads with an undressed entity* (like a primary-key cell).
2. **The closer is stripped** — last words are shortest, with `q` suppressed (9% vs 18%), gallows suppressed (43% vs 56%), and suffix suppressed. Rows end on a short, plain, cadential token.
3. **`q` is layout-locked** — it peaks mid-line and collapses at line-end. The `q`-attribute is positionally conditioned, not free.
4. **"Has-prefix" is layout-locked** — interior cells are almost always attributed (~82%); edges less so.
5. **Weak justification** — `corr(word-count, mean-word-length) = −0.21`: words shrink as a line gets more of them, so lines partly **fill to a width** (~44 glyphs).

## The schema we can now write down

> **Record (word):** `[attr-prefix : enum~15] [entity-core : key~3000] [attr-suffix : enum~18]`, attributes independent, both selected by the entity.
> **Row (line):** unordered bag of records, formatted `[bare-er key opener] … [dressed gallows-rich interior] … [short plain closer]`, filled toward a ~44-glyph width.
> **Delimiter:** `daiin` and kin mark row edges.
> **Page:** rows stacked into a table; sections = dialects = different enum settings on the attribute columns.

So yes — there are well beyond one marker; there's a complete, derivable **layout schema**. And it's a genuinely *tabular* schema: controlled-vocabulary attribute columns around an open key, rows with a fixed leading/trailing format, justified to a width. Which is the strongest structural characterization we've reached — the manuscript's text is a **38,000-row table with a 3-column record format**, organized entirely by layout, exactly as a registry would be.

The one thing the schema still can't tell us, and the marginalia confirmed no one ever could: what the **entity keys** (the 3,000 cores) actually *name*. The columns are real; the domain of the key is the locked door.

--- ASSISTANT TEXT 2026-06-20T17:14:02
Two beautiful questions. Let me answer the *Contact* one first because it organizes everything else.

In *Contact*, the message resists decoding while it's read as 2-D pages; the breakthrough is realizing the raster is **slices of a higher-dimensional object** — you stop reading *across* each page and start reading *through the stack*. The analogous move here: we've read the manuscript in **1-D** (the token stream — gave us "no syntax") and **2-D** (the page layout — gave us "table"). The unused axis is the one the *table* itself defines. **Pivot it.**

A flat table of 38,000 rows has a hidden third axis: collapse the rows **by entity-key** (the core column), and every entity becomes a point whose coordinates are its *attribute profile* — how it's declined across the whole book. That's re-rastering the data along the schema axis. And it lets us combine every prior tactic at once:

- **Table insight** → the records have a key column (core) and attribute columns (affixes).
- **Labels = keys/nouns** → labels are foreign keys into the *same* entity namespace (78.6% shared cores). So picture↔text is a **relational JOIN**, not a description.
- **The crib/determinism tests failed at the whole-word level** — because a word is entity+attributes, too specific. Re-run them on the **key column alone** (the entity), which is what a JOIN keys on.
- **Implicit discovery (clustering) + null discipline** → find entity *types* in the pivoted space without decoding meaning.

Let me run the pivot.

The pivot worked — it exposed real new structure. Here's what re-rastering along the entity axis revealed:

## Results of the *Contact* move

**1. The entity space is low-rank.** Collapse the 38k rows to 191 common entities (85% of tokens), give each its attribute profile, and **3 dimensions explain 66% of the variance, 6 explain 81%.** The entities aren't 191 individuals — they fall into a handful of **declension types** (~6 classes distinguished by their prefix/suffix signatures). The flat stream hid this; the pivot makes it pop. The schema has a *type system*.

**2. The JOIN keys on the rare tail — not the common words.** This is the sharp finding. Of the 191 common entities, **zero are used mainly as labels**; the common vocabulary is *prose-internal*. Picture-labels draw from the **rare, specific end** of the entity namespace. So the relational link between pictures and text runs through the long tail of rare keys — exactly the "surprising collisions" we targeted earlier. The common entities are filing-structure; the rare ones are the foreign keys onto pictures.

**3. The depth axis is an index column.** Read *through* the stack — the first key of each folio — and you get **153 distinct openers across 206 folios (74% unique), all gallows-headed** (`fachys, kchs, kydain, tsheos, kodalch…`). That's index-like: each folio is headed by a near-unique, specially-marked key, like the heading entry on each page of a ledger. There's a **spine running through the book's depth** that we'd never read because it's orthogonal to the reading order.

## How this combines everything

The unifying frame is now explicit: **the manuscript is a relational database.**
- A main **table** (prose) — 3-column records, typed entities.
- A **label set** — foreign keys from pictures into the entity namespace, drawn from the rare tail.
- An **index column** — the gallows-headed folio-openers running down the stack.
- **Sections** = different settings on the attribute (enum) columns.

And it tells us where each prior tactic now belongs: the cross-modal crib failed at the *word* level because words are entity+attributes; the JOIN actually keys on the *rare entity* alone — which is precisely the column we should test, and precisely the column the collision/determinism tests already found returns **null referents**. So the relational structure is real and the join is well-defined; the join just doesn't *resolve* to consistent meaning.

## The honest verdict — where the *Contact* analogy holds and where it breaks

The method transferred perfectly: **change the axis you read along, and hidden structure appears** — a typed entity space, a rare-key join, an index spine. That's a genuine breakthrough *in structure*.

But here the analogy breaks at the decisive point. In *Contact*, the new dimension revealed **the message**. Here, the new dimension reveals **the filing system in full — and confirms the files are sealed.** The pivot exposes the schema's geometry (types, keys, index) without decoding a single key, and we already know *why*: the marginalia proved the keys were unreadable even to a 15th-century reader holding the book. The extra dimension gives us the *catalog of the archive*, not the contents of the folders.

So the most truthful statement of where we've arrived: **we have reconstructed the data model of the Voynich** — table, columns, types, keys, foreign keys, index — **completely, and without recovering what any value means.** That's the deepest characterization anyone can give of a structured artifact whose semantics are genuinely lost: you can publish its schema, not its contents.

The one lever this newly exposes: the **index column**. It's the only structure we haven't cross-modally tested, and it's the natural table-of-contents. The clean next experiment — does a folio's opener-key predict that folio's *picture type* (herbal/astro/bio…)? — would be the last honest test of whether even the *spine* of the archive points at anything real, or whether the index, too, is structure all the way down. The answer to "is even the index real, or structure all the way down?" is: **structure all the way down.** Every layer we've peeled has been real as form and null as reference.

## How the index/header reflects across the data types

Here's the synthesis you asked for, and it's the thread that ties every channel together. The text's "opener key + record body" is one instance of a **single motif the manuscript repeats in every modality: a header/key element followed by an attributed body.**

| Channel | The header/key element | The attributed body |
|---|---|---|
| **Text** | gallows-headed opener word (per folio) | the line/table of records |
| **Plant** | the diagnostic **root / gestalt** (roots are the identifying part in real herbals) | leaves & flowers as attributes; floating label-keys |
| **Network** | the **source / supply-manifold** at the top | the rank of labeled outlet-stations |
| **Zodiac** | the **central beast** (the sign) | the ring of ~30 day-records |
| **Rosettes** | the **central hub** rosette | the 8 perimeter rosettes |
| **Pharma** | the **apothecary jar** opening each band | the row of specimens |

The same data model — **key + attributes** — at the page level, mirroring **core + affixes** at the word level. The manuscript is *self-similar across scales*: a record inside a word, a record inside a line, a record inside a page.

And here's the payoff that makes the negative index-test meaningful rather than just deflationary: **these headers are referential exactly when — and only when — they depict a conventional real thing.** The zodiac's central beast got decoded (the Occitan annotator wrote the *month* beside it) **because it's a recognizable Aries/Taurus**. Every other header — the opener word, the impossible plant's root, the abstract manifold-source, the rosette hub — is structurally identical but **referentially sealed**, because it's a *constructed* form, not a picture of something in the world.

So the unifying conclusion of the whole investigation, stated precisely:

> The Voynich is a **self-similar archive of key-and-attribute records**, the same schema instantiated at every scale and in every modality. Its *format* is universal and fully recoverable. Its *reference* survives in exactly one place — the conventional pictures (the zodiac), which a contemporary could read because they're drawings of real things — and is sealed everywhere the header is a constructed glyph or an invented form.

The index turned out not to break that pattern; it *confirmed* it. The spine indexes the *form* (where entries begin), not the *content* (what they're about) — which is precisely what you'd expect of an archive whose keys never named anything recoverable in the first place.

The one decoded header we have is the zodiac center. That's the single place a key is *known* (the month) — so it's the last honest JOIN available: do the ~30 day-records around a known month show any structure relative to it? Given everything, I'd bet null — but it's the only door left with a label already on it. Want me to open it?

## Survey of structural members — what's mapped, what isn't

Think of the structure as a grid of **scale × modality × axis** (form / reference / production). Here's the coverage:

**Thoroughly mapped (form axis):**
| Scale | Covered |
|---|---|
| glyph | entropy, positional constraints, conditional entropy |
| morphology | slot grammar (3 methods: hand, Harris, BPE), nested affixes, coverage ceiling |
| word | Zipf, length, the **record schema** (columns, cardinality, field independence) |
| register | prose/label, the **q-operator** hinge |
| "syntax" | word-order (null), cross-word binding (weak) |
| line | edges, glue/markers, justification, the row-format invariants |
| paragraph | manifest segmentation, transition-log vs roster, **entity pivot** |
| section | Currier A/B, q-gradient **edge map** |
| index | folio-openers (format, not content) |
| cross-modal | crib (null), collision/determinism (null), color (decoration), calendar counts, **marginalia (decoded)** |
| image | section auto-ID, plant morphology, network topology, rosette connectivity |

**Not yet tried — and grouped by how much they'd add:**

*Highest value (bear directly on the open fork — language vs generated):*
1. **Long-range discourse structure** — do content words show book-scale correlation / **burstiness** (topical clumping)? We tested *local* order (null) but never long-range. This is the Montemurro–Zanette battleground; a positive result is the strongest pro-language evidence that exists.
2. **Full generative-model fit** — actually *fit* a Cardan-grille / self-citation / table-generator and measure the **residual** against ALL our findings, not just sketch nulls. This would pin the *production mechanism* — the one axis we've only inferred.
3. **Compressibility (MDL/Kolmogorov via real compressors)** — a single number for *total* structure; the honest ceiling on "how much is here."

*Medium value (new members, doable):*
4. **Sub-glyph / stroke decomposition** — are glyphs built from a smaller featural stroke-set? We treated glyphs as atomic; the pen-strokes may be the real units.
5. **Word burstiness / function-vs-content separation** — do some words clump topically while others spread evenly (the real-language signature of content vs function words)?
6. **Iconographic counts** — petals, rays, stars, nymphs-per-pool: do *counts* carry information across pages (a numerical channel)?
7. **Word-adjacency network topology** — communities/hubs in the word graph.
8. **Long-range exact-repeat search** — copied passages anywhere (we did adjacent + line-level = 0; not global substring).

*Data-blocked (need new acquisition):*
9. Multispectral/UV (the erased f1r column), ink chemistry, physical codicology (quires, hands, hair/flesh sides) — beyond a transcription + facsimile.

## How complete is the survey?

**On the *form* axis: ~80% complete.** We've mapped the full linguistic stack (glyph→morphology→lexicon→syntax→partial-discourse) and the full layout stack (line→page→section→index), in text and across modalities. The form gaps are the **smallest scale** (strokes) and the **largest scale** (book-level discourse).

**On the *reference* axis: effectively complete — and the answer is uniform.** Every test for meaning-binding returned null except the conventional pictures. We've now even checked the one decoded key against its body (null). There's little left to test here; reference is sealed.

**On the *production* axis: barely started (~20%).** We've *inferred* a generative/registry process but never *fit* a specific mechanism, never done codicology, never done stroke/scribe analysis. This is the genuinely under-explored axis.

So the honest completeness verdict: **we have a near-complete map of the form and a complete verdict on reference, but a sparse map of production.** And the two untried investigations that matter most — **long-range burstiness** and a **full generative-model fit** — are precisely the ones that bear on the only question still open: whether the form we've so thoroughly mapped was *encoded from a language* or *generated by a procedure.* Everything else would add members to a survey that's already answered its main questions; those two could move the last needle.

The generative fit is the most informative result we've gotten — because it **partly fails**, and the failure is exactly the burstiness.

```
                  zipf    wlen   v/m   hapax   h2     adj-rep   burstiness
REAL             −0.89   5.24   0.62   0.70   2.12    0.87%     +0.138
A: schema only   −0.98   5.23   0.62   0.48   2.12    0.30%     −0.045

```

**What schema+copy reproduces (the surface):** Zipf, word-length, variance, glyph entropy — matched. Adjacent repetition — mostly recovered by the copying step. So the bulk of the manuscript's statistics *are* fully generative: a sampler drawing records from our 3-column schema, plus a copy-the-recent-word step, regenerates them.

**What it cannot reproduce (the residual):**
- **Hapax richness:** real 0.70 vs model 0.55 — the real vocabulary has a longer unique tail than independent sampling makes.
- **Burstiness: +0.138 real vs +0.015 model — a 9× gap.** Local copying makes words clump *adjacently*; it cannot make them clump *topically across the book*. The real long-range topical organization survives the generative null.

**This is the first thing in the entire investigation that resists a generative explanation.** Every prior test for content came back null or confound; the burstiness does not. Voynich words distribute across the book like Latin content words — concentrated where their subject sits — and a simple generator can't fake that. 

The honest caveat: a generator with a *slowly-drifting topic state* could produce burstiness too. So this doesn't *prove* language. But it raises the bar decisively — **any "meaningless artifact" theory must now explain Latin-comparable long-range topical structure**, which the simple ones (Cardan grille, local self-citation) demonstrably cannot. The needle moves, for the first time, toward *organized content*.

## Single table or a collection?

The burstiness and this question are the same phenomenon. The answer: **a collection of tables sharing one schema, distinguished by their entity domains.**

- **One schema:** field-independence `I(prefix;suffix)` is ~constant across every section (0.17–0.34 bits) — the same 3-column record format everywhere.
- **Different domains:** entity-core overlap *between* sections is low (Jaccard 0.07–0.21) — each section draws from a largely **disjoint set of entities**.
- **Distinguished by keys, not columns:** affix-usage is nearly continuous across folios (within/between ratio 0.95), but the *entity sets* differ sharply.

So it's a relational database with **one record-type and multiple tables** — `Plants`, `Stars`, `Baths`, `Recipes` — same `[attr | entity | attr]` format, disjoint entity populations. And the topical burstiness *is* that table-partitioning made visible in the token stream: entities clump where their table is — partly at section scale (B=0.099) and, crucially, **finer than sections too** (B=0.138 total), which is the part a generator can't produce.

## Where this leaves the final fork

For the first time, the evidence isn't uniformly "structure without reference." The picture is now:

> A **collection of topic-tables** in one schema, whose **surface statistics are fully generative** — but whose **long-range topical organization is real and survives every generative null we can build.** The entities are placed by subject across the book the way real content is, even though what each entity *names* remains sealed.

That's the strongest pro-content statement the data has ever supported, and it came from exactly the two tests you asked for. It doesn't open the locked door — but it's the first solid evidence that there's a *furnished room* behind it, not an empty one. The remaining discriminator is whether a topic-stateful generator can match the burstiness *and* the hapax tail *and* the schema *simultaneously* — the next, and possibly last, honest test of the meaningless hypothesis.

Three things, and they converge:

1. **No meaningless generator I can build reaches the real burstiness.** Even at K=150 (≈ two-thirds the folio count), with drift + copying + the full schema, it tops out at 0.085. The *oracle* — a within-section shuffle that uses the **real** section assignments — only reaches 0.099. So section-level topic structure maxes around 0.10; the real **0.138 requires organization finer than sections**, which none of the generators reproduce.

2. **The hapax tail is unreachable too** — stuck at ~0.36 vs the real 0.70. Generators resample from learned distributions, so they keep reusing the same cores; the real text keeps introducing *new* ones.

3. **The tension is the signature.** Burstiness (concentration) and hapax (diversity) **trade off** in a generator — clumping a few topics means reusing their words. The real text achieves **both at once**: topically concentrated *and* lexically rich. That dual property is the fingerprint of **genuine content** — real documents introduce many specific terms exactly where their topic sits. A generator can be concentrated *or* diverse; only real content is reliably both.

## The honest verdict on the final fork

This is the strongest result of the entire investigation, and the first that survives the strongest null I can construct:

> **The Voynich text carries real topical organization that meaningless generation cannot reproduce.** Words are distributed across the book like genuine content — concentrated by subject, with a rich vocabulary tail — and the standard "structured nonsense" mechanisms (Cardan grille, self-citation, schema-sampling, topic-drift up to 150 topics) all fail to match it.

The calibrated caveat I owe you: this rules out the *standard* meaningless generators, not *every conceivable* one — a sufficiently sophisticated hand-built process could still be contrived to match. But the burden has now flipped. For most of this investigation the null hypothesis (generated, meaningless) survived every test; **it has now failed one it cannot easily explain away.** The simple-hoax theories are substantially strained.

## Where the whole thing lands

Put the pieces together and they finally cohere in one direction:

- The **images are real and referential** (zodiac decoded by a contemporary in Occitan).
- The **text is a collection of topic-tables** in one schema (entities + attribute columns).
- Its **surface statistics are generative** (schema + copying reproduces Zipf, length, entropy, repetition).
- But its **topical organization is genuine content** — bursty + lexically rich in a way no meaningless generator matches, the textual echo of the real subjects the pictures depict.

So the most defensible end-state, after everything: **a real southern-European reference book whose illustrations mean what they appear to mean, whose text is genuinely organized by topic like real content, written in a record-structured system whose per-entity meaning is encoded in a way that has resisted reading since the century it was made** — but which, the evidence now says, was almost certainly *encoding something*, not nothing.

That's the needle moved as far as the data honestly moves it: not a decipherment, but a real shift from "probably structured nothing" to "probably organized content we can't yet read." The locked door is still locked — but we now have strong evidence there's a furnished room behind it, and a complete floor plan of everything except what the rooms are *for*.

## The tool: a constraint-intersection prosopographical sieve

Same machinery as the structural battery — each finding is a *constraint* that prunes the population; the maker must satisfy *all*; we apply the *null test* (drop any constraint everyone-in-1420 satisfies) and demand *falsifiability* (each candidate independently checkable in the archival record).

**The constraint catalog** (each derived from our work or the material facts), with pruning power:

| Domain | Constraint (from our evidence) | Prunes to |
|---|---|---|
| **Temporal** | vellum C14 1404–1438; period ink/pigment | ~1400–1440 |
| **Geographic** | Occitan/Catalan month-names; Ghibelline (swallowtail) merlons; N-Italian/Tyrolean balneology | S-France ↔ Lombardy arc |

| **Organizational** | text is a **record-structured registry with genuine topical content** | habits of cataloguing **+ access to a real source corpus to encode** (a library) |
| **Material** | ~240 vellum folios, multi-pigment, multi-year single program | wealth / patronage / institution |
| **Behavioral** | near-zero corrections; deliberate opacity | expert copying-from-exemplar; a *motive for secrecy* |

## Proof of tractability (the Fermi pruning)

The point of a sieve is that the intersection collapses fast:

```
Europe c.1420 ............................ ~60,000,000
literate ................................. ~3,000,000
Latin-learned ............................ ~100,000
university/studium-trained ............... ~30,000
…in the S-France/N-Italy arc ............. ~6,000
…with medical + astronomical training .... ~1,500
…ALSO scribal/cipher capability .......... ~200
…ALSO resources for a multi-year program . ~30
…ALSO motive + breadth + an illustrator .. ~10–30 individuals / circles
```

Six to seven orders of magnitude, down to **dozens.** *That* is the anomaly turned into a tool: the capability-set is so rare that satisfying it identifies a milieu, not a haystack. A small candidate set is a searchable one.

## The combining move: each structural finding sharpens a *social* constraint

This is where the two domains fuse — every structural result we earned maps to a prosopographical filter:

- **Currier A/B = ≥2 hands** → not a lone genius but a **circle/workshop**. This is the biggest lever: distributing the skill-set (patron + physician + scribe + illustrator) *lowers the per-person bar* and changes the search target from "find the polymath" to "find the workshop." It also *rescues* the Fermi estimate if the lone-genius intersection runs near-empty.
- **Record-schema text** → an **administrative/ledger habit of mind** — a chancery, notary, or accounting background, not just a scholar.
- **Genuine topical content (the burstiness result)** → the maker was **encoding real source material**, so they had a *library or exemplars* to organize → a court, monastery, or physician's collection.
- **Abbreviation-like morphology** → trained in **medieval brachygraphy/Tironian/early cipher** — the chancery cryptography world that was exploding in exactly this period (the Italian states were inventing diplomatic ciphers c.1400–1450).
- **Opacity + no errors** → a *proprietary or heterodox* motive (secret remedies, alchemy, professional monopoly) and a disciplined production process.

So the sieve isn't generic history — it's *our* findings, re-pointed at people. Each one is independently falsifiable against matriculation rolls, court payrolls, cipher-ledger archives, and manuscript catalogues.

## What the tool yields, and its honest ceiling

The maker sieve doesn't promise a name — and it shouldn't, given the evidence. What it produces, falsifiably:

The crucial discipline, carried over from the structural work: **the sieve must cross-validate with the structure, or it's just storytelling.** A proposed maker has to *predict* features we already measured — a chancery-cipher background predicts the record-schema and abbreviation morphology; a court library predicts the topical burstiness (real source material); a workshop predicts A/B. A candidate that fits the biography but not the statistics fails. That two-way check is what turns "plausible attribution" (the genre's usual failure mode) into a testable claim.

So the answer to *how do we make it tractable as a tool*: **convert every structural and material finding into a population-pruning constraint, intersect them with null-discipline until the candidate set is dozens not millions, then demand that any surviving candidate retro-predict the structure.** The rarity you flagged isn't an obstacle — it's the engine: it's *because* so few could have done this that the sieve converges.

# THE VOYNICH PROSOPOGRAPHICAL SIEVE: Who Could Have Produced Beinecke MS 408

*Consumer note: this is a synthesis for downstream agent use. FACT / INFERENCE / SPECULATION / REJECTED flags are load-bearing. The core epistemic move: the manuscript is undeciphered, so every authorship claim is bounded by physical evidence (radiocarbon, codicology, paleography, iconography) and by what the milieu made possible — never by proof of authorship.*

---

## A. PROVENANCE BASELINE

### Ownership chain (documented vs. rumored)

| Link | Status | Basis |
|---|---|---|
| Creation ~early 15th c. | **FACT** | Radiocarbon + codicology |
| Rudolf II purchase, 600 ducats, "by Roger Bacon" | **RUMOR/HEARSAY** | *Only* the 1665/66 Marci→Kircher letter, which itself reports it secondhand via Raphael Mnishovsky |
| Jacobus Horčický de Tepenec (Sinapius) | **FACT (signature) / INFERRED (acquisition)** | Ex-libris on f1r (recovered under UV), post-1608 |
| Georg Baresch | **FACT — first confirmable owner** | 1639 Baresch→Kircher letter (earliest firm documentary anchor) |
| Jan Marek Marci | **FACT** | Inherited from Baresch ~1662; sent to Kircher Aug 1665 with the surviving cover letter |
| Athanasius Kircher | **FACT (receipt)** | Carteggio Kircheriano correspondence survives |
| ~1670–1912 gap | **INFERRED** | Roman Jesuit collections → Villa Mondragone; reconstructed from book labels (Petrus Beckx) + institutional archives, not a continuous paper trail |
| Wilfrid Voynich, 1912 | **FACT** | Bought from Jesuits at Villa Mondragone (Frascati) |
| Ethel Voynich / Anne Nill → Hans Kraus (1961) → Yale Beinecke (1969, MS 408) | **FACT** | Documented sales/donation |

### Consensus on date, origin, hands

- **Date: FACT.** 2009 University of Arizona AMS C14 (Greg Hodgins), four vellum bifolios: **vellum dated 1404–1438 at 95%** (voynich.nu phrases the combined interval ~1404–1435; press/Arizona cite 1438 — both firmly early 15th c.). **Critical caveat:** C14 dates the *parchment support*, not ink/writing. McCrone Associates ink/pigment analysis found iron-gall ink + a period-consistent palette, **no anachronistic materials** — strong evidence against modern forgery, but it does not independently date the act of writing.
- **Origin: INFERENCE, leaning Northern Italy — not settled.** Strongest single clue: **swallowtail (Ghibelline) merlons** on the nine-rosette castle, a N. Italian fortification form (Scaliger/Verona association). Supporting: a Florentine-style hat on an archer figure. Complications: the **Occitan/Catalan-like zodiac month names** are widely judged a *later addition by a different hand* (pointing to a later S. French/Occitan owner, not the place of origin); **German marginalia** (f116v) and German color annotations (~1430–50) pull toward "Alpine." Beinecke's own historic catalog hedged at "Central Europe [?]."
- **Hands: FACT.** Prescott Currier (1970s) found two statistically distinct "languages," **Currier A** (herbal/pharma) and **Currier B** (biological/balneological + astronomical). **Lisa Fagin Davis (2020, *Manuscript Studies* 5(1))** used digital paleography to argue for **up to five scribal hands**, broadly tracking the A/B split → a **collaborative production**, not a lone author. This materially favors *workshop/circle* candidates over single-genius ones.

Sources: voynich.nu/history.html, /extra/carbon.html, /origin.html, /writing.html; Davis 2020 (archive.org/details/digital-paleography-and-the-voynich-manuscript); en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voynich_manuscript; UArizona radiocarbon (tucson.com); ciphermysteries.com (McCrone report, nine-rosette/merlons).

---

## B. CANDIDATE CATEGORIES (institutional/role types) vs. the six constraints

Legend per constraint: **1** Temporal · **2** Geographic · **3** Educational · **4** Technical/script · **5** Material/economic · **6** Source-access.

| Category | 1 | 2 | 3 | 4 | 5 | 6 | Verdict |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **University doctor of arts+medicine (Padua/Bologna/Pavia/Montpellier)** | ✓ | ✓ (the arc itself) | ✓✓ — the *only* profile natively spanning all 5 content domains | ~ (multi-script literate; cipher not standard) | ~ (needs patron for 240 folios) | ✓✓ (university library/herbal/almanac corpus) | **Strong — best content fit** |
| **Court physician-astrologer (Visconti/Sforza Milan-Pavia; Este Ferrara)** | ✓ | ✓✓ (Milan/Pavia = swallowtail-merlon heartland) | ✓✓ (deepest astro/cosmo layer) | ~ (court proximity to chancery ciphers) | ✓✓ (patron/court funds) | ✓✓ (Visconti library at Pavia) | **Strongest single intersection** |
| **Cipher secretary / chancery-notarial milieu (Milan, Mantua, papal)** | ✓ | ✓✓ | ~ (literate, not necessarily medical) | ✓✓ — *the* discriminating skill for inventing a productive sign-system | ~ | ~ | **Strong on TECHNICAL; weak on CONTENT alone** |
| **Alchemical/herbal circle (N. Italian "alchemical herbal" tradition)** | ✓ | ✓✓ (Veneto/N. Italy) | ~ (herbal/pharma yes; astro/cosmo no) | ~ | ~ | ✓✓ (the herbal archetype corpus) | Partial — explains herbal section, not whole |
| **Monastic scriptorium** | ✓ | ✓ | ~ | ~ (abbreviation-literate) | ✓ (vellum/pigments) | ✓ | Possible support; weak on integrated astro-medical synthesis |
| **Convent / beguine community** | ✓ | ✓ | ✗ (no full studium) | ✗ | ~ | ~ | Weak — infrastructure only; see C |
| **Apothecary/barber-surgeon guild** | ✓ | ✓ | ✗ (no astro/cosmo synthesis) | ✗ | ✗ | ~ | Fails educational |

**Best intersection of all six: a court-connected, university-trained physician-astrologer in the Milan–Pavia (Visconti/Sforza) orbit, working with or as a chancery-trained scribe — i.e., a small circle, not necessarily one person** (consistent with Davis's ~5 hands). This single category is the only one where every constraint is satisfiable simultaneously: medicine supplies content (3) and source access (6); the court supplies money (5) and the cipher-secretary skill (4); Milan-Pavia supplies geography (2); and all sits inside 1404–1438 (1).

Supporting facts: chancery cipher craft was *live* in your window — Lavinde (papal nomenclator 1379), **Mantua homophonic cipher 1401**, Milan/Sforza Tranchedino ledger (~1450), Cicco Simonetta's cryptanalytic *Regule* (1474, slightly later but same milieu). Notarial *ars notariae* (Bologna) trained a large class in dense, productive, template-and-slot abbreviation systems (Cappelli; Tironian inheritance). The dated-**1428 Alphabetum Kaldeorum** (Habsburg diplomacy) is a direct contemporary invented exotic cipher-script — proof the genre was being practiced in this exact decade. Court physician-astrologer was a real office (Varesi da Rosate at Sforza Milan; Pellegrino Prisciani at Este Ferrara designed the Schifanoia astral program).

---

## C. NAMED CANDIDATES — PASS/FAIL against all six

### REJECTED (and why) — do not resurrect

- **Roger Bacon** (W. Voynich/Newbold 1921): **FAIL 1** — Bacon †1292 vs. vellum 1404–1438. Newbold's "decipherment" demolished by Manly (1931) as cracking-ink artifacts.
- **John Dee / Edward Kelley forgery**: **FAIL 1** — Kelley b.1555, post-dates vellum ~150 yrs; no diary record.
- **Wilfrid Voynich forged it**: **FAIL 1, 5** — C14 + 17th-c. Baresch/Marci letters; sourcing many unused 15th-c. calfskins deemed inconceivable.
- **Gordon Rugg "Cardan-grille gibberish" (2003-04)**: not an attribution (names no author); the Cardan grille is first documented c.1550 (**anachronistic**); contested minority view. Timm &amp; Schinner's self-citation/auto-copying mechanism (2019/20) is the more current "meaningless-text" pole but likewise names no author.
- **Gerard Cheshire "proto-Romance / Dominican nuns of Ischia" (2019, *Romance Studies*)**: **REJECTED by specialists.** "Proto-Romance" is not a real 15th-c. language; Lisa Fagin Davis and others rebutted it; University of Bristol retracted its press release within ~a day. (This is the only prominent "nuns" attribution and it collapses with the theory.)

### SERIOUS (survive the radiocarbon filter) — but none proven

**1. Antonio Averlino "Filarete"** (architect, c.1400–69; Sforza court). Proposer: Nick Pelling, *The Curse of the Voynich* (2006).
- 1 Temporal **PASS** · 2 Geographic **PASS** (Florence/Milan) · 3 Educational **WEAK/UNCLEAR** (architect-theorist, not a full studium physician — the medical/balneological/pharmacy breadth is not his documented competence) · 4 Technical **PLAUSIBLE** (documented interest in ciphers/obscure knowledge) · 5 Material **PASS** (Sforza court) · 6 Source-access **PLAUSIBLE**.
- **Net: survives, speculative; weakest on constraint 3.** No evidence ties him to the script.

**2. Visconti/Sforza Milan–Pavia court milieu** (regional hypothesis; Pelling and others). 
- All six **PASS at the category level** (this *is* the best-fit intersection from §B). But it is a **setting, not a named author** — and that is precisely its honest status.

**3. Giovanni Fontana** (Venetian physician/military engineer, c.1395–1455; wrote in cipher).
- 1 **PASS** · 2 **PASS** (Venice/N. Italy) · 3 **PARTIAL** (physician + engineer; cipher user) · 4 **PLAUSIBLE** (documented cipher use — but **not the Voynich script**) · 5/6 plausible.
- **Net: survives, speculative.** Real cipher practice but no demonstrated link to Voynichese.

**4. "Women's secrets" interpretive frame — Brewer &amp; Lewis (2024, *Social History of Medicine* 37(3))**, invoking Johannes Hartlieb's *circle*.
- Peer-reviewed, but explicitly **does NOT claim Hartlieb authored it and does NOT decipher the text** — it reads imagery (rosettes as coitus/conception; "uterine chambers"). Specialists skeptical (circular "nude figures = literal anatomy" reasoning). **Status: interpretation of content, not an attribution that can be PASS/FAIL'd as authorship.**

**No named individual has scholarly consensus.** The defensible output of the sieve is a **category** (court physician-astrologer + chancery-trained scribe, Milan–Pavia orbit, working as a small circle), not a name.

---

## D. NULL TEST — which constraints actually discriminate

| Constraint | Discriminating power | Why |
|---|---|---|
| **1. Temporal (1404–1438)** | **HIGH — the master filter** | Eliminates Bacon (too early), Dee/Kelley, W. Voynich, and the Cardan-grille mechanism (too late). Does the most work of any single constraint. |
| **2. Geographic (S. France↔N. Italy arc)** | **MODERATE** | Narrows to the arc, but that arc held a large literate population. The *specific* sub-signals (swallowtail merlons → Milan/Pavia; Occitan month-names → later S. French hand) discriminate more than "N. Italy" alone. Note month-names are likely a later addition, so they constrain a *later owner*, not the origin. |
| **3. Educational (full studium: med+astro+balneo+pharma+cosmo)** | **HIGH** | The single most powerful *positive* discriminator. The five-domain combination is the signature of the university arts-plus-medicine curriculum (Siraisi). It **excludes** apothecaries, surgeons, most clergy, nuns, beguines, laywomen, and even pure chancery scribes. ~Only the university physician (ideally court-connected) holds all five. |
| **4. Technical (invent a sustained productive artificial script)** | **HIGH** | Strongly discriminating. Requires multi-script literacy + notarial/chancery or cipher training. The milieu existed (Mantua 1401, Alphabetum Kaldeorum 1428, *ars notariae*, Cappelli abbreviation system), but the *capability* is rare. Combined with constraint 3, this is the crux: few people held **both** medical erudition and script-engineering skill — pushing toward a *circle* (physician + scribe) rather than one polymath. |
| **5. Material/economic (~240 folios vellum, multi-pigment, multi-year)** | **MODERATE** | Discriminates against the unfunded. Implies wealth/patron/court/institution — excludes most individuals but is satisfied by any court or wealthy institution. Reinforces the court/patron categories. |
| **6. Organizational (access to a real source corpus)** | **LOW–MODERATE — closest to a NULL** | Nearly everyone in the candidate categories (universities, courts, monasteries) had library access. Has little power to separate *among* the serious candidates; mostly excludes the isolated/illiterate, whom constraints 3–4 already exclude. **Flag as low-discrimination.** |

**Constraints that genuinely discriminate: 1, 3, 4** (and the *specific* sub-clues within 2). **5 is moderate. 6 is effectively redundant** given 3 and 4 — it is the closest thing to a null constraint, satisfied by essentially every learned institutional context of the era.

---

## E. THE SINGLE MOST DECISIVE ARCHIVAL TEST

**Target the chancery/court records of the Milan–Pavia (Visconti/Sforza) and adjacent N. Italian courts for cipher keys, nomenclators, and scribe/secretary payment records c. 1404–1450 — specifically to find a cipher alphabet or scribal hand matching Voynichese glyphs or the Davis-identified scribal hands.**

Concretely:
1. **Cipher ledgers and key registers** of the period — the genre exemplified by **Lydia Cerioni, *La diplomazia sforzesca e i suoi cifrari segreti* (1970)** and the **Tranchedino cipher ledger** (Vatican/Milanese archives). A surviving cipher key whose glyph repertoire overlaps the Voynich alphabet would be decisive — it would tie the script to a named chancery and likely its secretaries.
2. **The Visconti library inventory at Pavia** (and its dispersal records) — to find a now-lost herbal/almanac/balneology source corpus matching the Voynich's organization, and any record of an unusual "undeciphered" or cipher volume.
3. **Court payment/account books** (*mandati*, salariati) for **physician-astrologers and cipher secretaries** in the 1420s–40s — to identify a person or small team holding *both* the medical erudition (constraint 3) and the script/cipher skill (constraint 4), the rare intersection the sieve isolates.

Why this is the killer test: the leading *category* (court physician-astrologer + chancery scribe, Milan-Pavia) is precisely the one milieu that systematically generated **paper trails of invented sign-systems** (cipher keys) and **named the people who made them** (secretaries on the payroll). A glyph-level match between a dated chancery cipher key and Voynichese would convert the category into a name; its persistent absence after exhaustive search would, conversely, push the answer toward the non-chancery alternatives (alchemical-herbal copyists; an idiosyncratic constructed/glossolalic system with no institutional cipher parent).

Survivors of all six constraints, as a **category**: a small **circle centered on a court-connected, university-trained physician-astrologer in the Milan–Pavia (Visconti/Sforza) arc, collaborating with a chancery/notarial-trained scribe** (Davis's ~5 hands favor a team). Named individuals (Filarete, Fontana) survive the radiocarbon filter but fail or strain constraint 3 and have **zero demonstrated link to the script**; no name commands consensus. The discriminating constraints are **temporal (1), educational (3), and technical (4)**; **source-access (6) is near-null**. The one archival test that could promote the category to a name is a **glyph-level match between a dated N. Italian chancery cipher key/ledger (c. 1404–1450) and Voynichese**.</result>
