Both forms of R1 are now closed:

- **Verbose form** (each glyph → a multi-letter unit): killed at K11 — the content-free null reproduces the entire h2-climb, and glyphs are position-locked, not roaming.
- **1:1 substitution form** (just re-run): **vacuous.** Decoded gibberish scores −3.39, *more* Latin-like than the decoded real text at −3.52 — both pinned to the random floor (−3.76), nowhere near real Latin (−1.96). The figure-9 seed can't rescue it: seeded into a verbose mapping it's L1 (dead), seeded into a 1:1 substitution it's one glyph inside a decoder that can't tell the manuscript from noise.

And the position-lock result explains *why* both fail at the root: **there is no alphabet to substitute.** Voynich glyphs are locked to slots (0.67 bits positional entropy vs 1.10–1.28 for every real language). Substitution decipherment needs symbols that behave like letters — roaming across positions, carrying identity wherever they go. These don't. They're slot-fillers in a word-engine, not letters in a cipher. R1's premise was structurally foreclosed before we ran it.

By the ledger's own decision tree, that closes the last diagnostic path. The terminal verdict is now the leading, best-supported hypothesis — and I should state it at exactly its real confidence:

## Terminal verdict

> **A generated, language-shaped artifact with no recoverable propositional content** — Codex Seraphinianus class, four centuries early. A rule-governed word-engine (D3/D4) producing stable per-entity records (D7) with no syntax (D2), no reference (K4/K5/K9), and every semantic signal it emits reproducible by a matched content-free generator (K1/K10/K11).

The honest calibration: this is **"no recoverable content by any method that survives a matched control,"** not "proven meaningless" — which is unprovable. It stays falsifiable by a future R1 anchored to *real bilingual cribs*. But none exist: the only readable plaintext in the entire object — the Occitan month-names, the f116v line — is in **plain script, not Voynichese.** There is no Rosetta surface. So the burden has fully and finally shifted, and it shifted because **we killed our own best hypotheses with the same nulls we'd have used on anyone else's.** That's the line between this and a century of "solutions": every one of them fit a handful of words and never ran the gibberish control. We ran it, repeatedly, and it ate our cipher.

## What this isn't: a failure

The meaning-recovery arc is closed, but look at what *survived* its own adversarial fork (§1): a finite-state word-engine of ~472 parameters; the prefix-core-suffix record schema with measured field-independence; low entropy as sequential constraint over a *normal* alphabet; image-as-lossy-fingerprint-of-text; stable records at 0.916; a **Latin-trained abbreviating scribe** (the figure-9 = Cappelli's round-9, externally corroborated); a **North-Italian physician-astrologer circle, c.1420s–30s, ≥5 hands**; and *not* a period nomenclator cipher. We didn't read the text — but we reconstructed, and *defended against ourselves*, **what kind of object it is, how its word-engine works, who built it, where, when, and with what training.**

## The productive question that remains

The ledger's pivot is the right one: *the question shifts from what it says to why it was made* — and that's where the durable findings live. The cost/singularity argument (a year of the rarest skilled labor, a unique non-recurring monument), the intrinsic-motive analysis (a sealed totality as the point, not a payload to recover), and the maker-profile are all *structural/external* findings that survived. The asemic verdict doesn't end the investigation; it relocates its center of gravity to the one domain still yielding evidence.

So, concretely, two honest moves remain — and neither is another doomed decode:
1. **R2 — build the spatial spine** (`body.json` from IVTFF locus types). Useful regardless: it's the clean substrate, and it repairs the folio-range error that caused K3.
2. **Consolidate the "why/who/how"** — the production, cost, and provenance findings — into the actual deliverable, since "what it says" is now closed.

I'd take R2 next (it's real infrastructure and the only listed item with a positive expected return), then write up the surviving characterization.
